EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 1230
Asuncion, Paraguay, October 27, 1945
Subject: No Action Required in Paraguay Regarding
Japanese Gotternment Archives and Property.
SECHET
The Honorable,
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D.C.
Sir:
With reference to the Department's circular telegram of
October 25, 8 a.m., and previous telegram regarding the action to
be taken with respect to Japanese diplomatic and consular archives
and official property, and to this Embassy's unrestricted despatch
no. 1220 of October 24, 1945, subject: "Japanese Diplomatic and
Consular Archives in Paraguay", I have the honor to report that
00
in the Embassy's opinion, since the Ministry of Foreign Relations
states that there are no Japanese diplomatic or consular archives
or property in Paraguay, it will not be ne cessary for this Embassy
to take any further action in this connection.
Respectfully yours,
Willa rd L. Beaulac

139, October 27, 4. p.m.. :
At a mecting at the British Embassy this
morning called by the British Ambassador by
invitation issued October 24 attended by British,
American, Chinese and Soviet Ambassadors, there
were discussed possible methods of addressing the
Netherlands Government on the subject of access to
Japanese archives if and when in possession of
that Government.
Agreement was reached that the four Ambassadors
would telegraph to their Governemts,text of draft
note which is self-explanatory, which involves a pro-
cedure they recommend, and which reads as follows:
"(to the Netherlands Minister for Foreign Affairs).
I have the honor by direction of His Majesty's
principal Secretary of State for Foreign ffairs to
POR
inform youthat

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.98)
293
SECRET
-2-#139, October 27,. 4 p.m.. from The Hague
inform you that His Majesty's Government in the
United Kingdom, in concert with the Governments
of the United States, China and the Soviet Union,
are anxious to be given an opportunity for their
representatives to examine any Hapanese diplomatic
and consular archives which have come or may in the
future come into the hands of the Netherlands
Government (note: At this point the draft oricinally
submitted by the British Ambassador contained in
addition a clause, whether in the Netherlands
metropolitan territory or in the Netherlands West
Indies.! After considerable discussion that clause
was dropped).
I am accordingly instructed to express the hope
that the Netherlands Governnent will be good enough
to share the right of access to such archives with
the Governments above mentioned.
I have the honour to add that my United Statecs,
Chinese and Soviet colleagues associate themselves
with me in putting forward this request."
I request authorization to inform British
Ambassador this proposed procedure approved.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
STANDARD FORM NO. 64
TO
NE - Mr. Colquitt
DATE: Ootober 27, 1945
FROM
Mr. Wadsworth
SUBJECT:
As stated in Mr. Straus' memorandum to you dated
September 28, my action in taking possession of the
Japanese consular arohives and miscellaneous bits of
property left in custody of the protecting power
This Document Must Be Retuiled 
(Argentina and, later, Spain) in Beirut was taken in
strict conformity with the Department's instruotions.
The latter directed me when taking such action
to request my British, Russian, and Chinese colleagues
where present to associate themselves with me. There
being no Chinese representative in Beirut, I put this
request to my British and Russian colleagues, who ex-
pressed themselves as happy to permit me to aot in
their names.
There was, in my inatructions, no mention of France
as either a "victorious power" or as an ally with whose
representative in Beirut it was desired that I act in
the matter.
As a matter of courtesy, however, immediately
after taking possession of the archives and property
in question, I sent Third Secretary Schoellkopf to
inform the French Delegation General of my action. I
also informed the Lebanese authorities thereof, having
already obtained the Prime Minister's informal approval
in advance of taking action.
Also, when having Schoellkopf call at the Delegation
General, I had him enquire as to whether any Japanese
property was held by the French-controlled "Sequestre"
(Alien Property Custodian). At the time of my recent
departure from Beirut, no reply had been received to
that enquiry; nor was I optimistic that such reply
would be received, for enquiries in the matter made by
CJC
my Soviet colleague had also received no reply and the
whole matter of the control of the "Sequestre" was under
discussion between the French and Lebanese authorities -
994.414/1
the French arguing, according to the Lebanese Foreign
Minister, that they could not transfer responsibility
DC/R
in this matter because it was one assumed by them as a
belligerent power.
A17
I have
Cat

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
I have gone into this detail because there is a
somewhat related matter which you may wish to raise in
your reply to the French Embassy's memorandum. It is
the matter of the German consular archives and property
now held by the Sequestre.
These German archives and property came into the
hands of the Sequestre some months ago when the Swiss
Consul relinquished representation of German interests.
The relinquishment was complicated by the fact that
Switzerland does not recognize Lebanese independence;
hence the Consul simply informed the Lebanese authorities
thereof informally and as an act of courtesy.
As to this matter you will recall an exchang• of
telegrams and airgrams with me at Beirut. As I read
your instructions, you desired that these German archives,
etc. be placed under the control or at least made available
to the representatives of the United States, U.S.S.R.,
Great Britain and France.
When taking them, however, the French in Beirut
gave no notice to me or to my Russian or British colleagues.
I suggested that you consider asking our Embassy in Paris
to have appropriate instructions in the matter sent by
the Quai d'Orsay to the Delegation General in Beirut. I
have not heard what action in the matter was taken.
To recapitulate: (1) I think the French Embassy
need only be told that the action taken in Beirut with
respect to the Japanese consular archives and property
was in full accord with the determined practice of the
powers victorious over Japan (United States, U.S.S.R.,
Great Britain, and China); and (2) that we should wel-
come information as to whether the French authorities
in Beirut have been instructed in the matter of the
German consular archives and property to act in accord
with the determined practice of the powers victorious
over Germany (United States, U.S.S.R., Great Britain,
and France).
George Wadsworth
NE:GWadsworth:dlc
10/30/45

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
NO. 3341
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF ÁMERICA
Rio de Janeiro, October 30., 1945.
File
SUBJECT: Japanese Archives and Japanese Intelligence
Activities.
2-4-46
The Honorable
The Seoretary of State,
Washington, D.C.
Sir:
As of interest in connection with the matter
the Japanese archives in Brazil, which has been the
subject of correspondence between the Department and
894.414/10-3045
the Embassy, I have the honor to enclose a copy of a
secret memorandum, dated October 26, 1945, prepared
by the Legal Attaché of the Embassy, reporting an
interview recently had with Suetaka Hayao, formerly
a secretary of the Japanese Embassy at Rio de Janeiro.
Apparently the Swedish Legation at Rio has not
yet received instructions to turn over the Japanese
archives now in its oustody. The Embassy is aware of
no change in the Brazilian Government's attitude as
expressed by the Seoretary General of the Foreign Of-
fice to the effect that the Swedish Legation would
turn over the Japanese archives to the Ministry of
Foreign Affairs (see Embassy's telegrams Nos. 2574 of
August 17, 7 PM, and 2588 of August 18, 2 PM). Judg-
ing from the Department's recent circular telegram of
October 25, 8 AM, the Swedish Legation should receive
instructipns to turn over the archives in the near
future; and at that time the Embassy will be better
able to establish a procedure for examining them in
cooperation with the Brazilien Ministry of Foreign
Affairs.
CS/VJ
Respectfully yours,
warned
FEE
Adolf A. Berle, Jr.
Enclosurea
Copy of memorandum from Legal Attaché.
File No. 701/820.02

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Sole enclosure to Despatoh No. 3341
of Oetober 30, 1945 from the Embassy
at Rio de Janeiro. Brazil.
MEMORANDUM FOR THE AMBASSADOR
Attention: Mr. Daniels
RO: SUETAKA HAYAO
For your information regarding the above Japanese
national who formerly was a secretary in the Japanese
Embassy in Rio de Janeiro and until recently repre-
sented Japanese interests in the Spanish Embassy, it
is advised that HAYAo was interviewed recently at the
Rio de Janeiro Police Department by a representative
of this office regarding his knowledge of Japanese
espionage activities in Brazil and South America
during the recent world confliot. HAYAo denied cate-
gorically having participated in any such activities
and expressed his doubts that the Japanese government
had set up any organized intelligence system other
than that operated by the Embassy and the various
Attachés of the Japanese armed forces.
On the basis of an arrangement made at the Police
Department, HAYAO called at the Embassy a few days
later in order that his assistance could be secured
in decoding a message which was known to have been
transmitted to him from Buenos Aires through a local
Japanese firm shortly after the rupture of diplomatic
relations. HAYAo denied being able to decode this
document and said that he had not been provided with
any coding systems or equipment at the time of the
general repatriation of Axis diplomats. He stated
that in the event the message in question had come
into his possession, he would have communicated with
the Japanese Embassy in Buenos Aires through the
Spanish Embassy in an open communication in which he
would request olarirication of the suspect message.
Inasmuoh as this message was tranamitted for HAYAO
through a Japanese employee of the firm, "Nipo-Brasi-
leira" (presently under liquidation), one RYOKICHI
NAGAI, the latter has been located in São Paulo and
will be questioned in an effort to obtain informa-
tion in his possession regarding the suspect message
and Japanese intelligenoe activities.
As of possible interest to you, HAYAo, who
speaks reasonably good English and fluent Portuguese,
claimed that the career diplomatic corps of the
Japanese government was opposed to the recent war
and that he, himself, was convinoed from the begin-
ning that Japan would be defeated by the United States.
He also mentioned the fact that it had come to his
attention through the Swedish Legation, which repre-
sented Japanese interests since Spain discontinued
diplomatio
SECRET

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRET
- 2 -
diplomatio relations with Japan, that the United States
Embassy was knowm to have made representations to the
Brazilian Foreign Office toward gaining access to the
Japanese Embassy files. HaYAo stated that he under-
stood the Foreign Office had not insisted that the
Swedish Legation deliver the files and had stated that
the final decision regarding them would be made by the
United States Embassy.
HAYAo inquired as to what information the United
States Embassy might be seeking in the files and said
he thought he might be able to supply such information
and thus obviate the necessity of examining the files.
It would appear that the possibility of examination
of these files caused him some concern, and it was
with apparent satisfaotion that he remarked that the
Swedish Legation had refused thus far to deliver them
to the Foreign Office.
No comment was made to HAYAO regarding the Embas-
sy's interest in the files other than the fact that
such a procedure has been general as has been announced
in the local press.
Respectfully,
Heber M. Clegg
Legal Attache.
October 26, 1945.
SECRET

(except in the case of the Potsdam Powers, the

sent to His Majesty's Representatives in countries
concerned, who have been instructed that in the
event of objection to the transfer of the Japanese
premises and archives being raised by any govern-
ment, they should not press the matter beyond
requesting permission to examine Japanese archives.
Thus there is a difference of view between
us on this one aspect, and I am of course entirely
at your disposal should you wish to discuss it.

Memorandum of Conversation
TELEPHONE
August 19, 1946
DATE:
SUBJECT:
Revision of procedure for handling Japanese property and
archives.
PARTICIPANTS: Colonel GrOSS, A-H
Mr. Lory, JA
SPICIAL PHOIECTS
Mr. Williams, SPD
DIVISION
AUG 2 0 194
COPIES TO:
1—1493
Colonel Gross called today to say that he had SPD's
draft telegram to London, Nanking and Moscow on this subject
and questioned only one point, namely that the Japanese
were to be told "by direction of" the Allied Powers of China,
UK, US, and USSR that such and such a procedure would be
adopted. He pointed out that it was no longer proper for
these four governments alone to direct SCAP and suggested
substitution of the word "agreement" in place of "direction".
ir. Williams said that he agreed with Colonel Gross,
and said that he would like to clear the change, even though
slight, with JA anu Fe. Colonel Gross agreed. Mr. Williams
then called Mr. Lory of JA who agreed to the change without
demur and said that he would also guarantee Fe's concurrence.
Mr. Williams then called Colonel Gross back and told
him that the change seemed to be thoroughly approved. The
Colonel thanked Mr. Williams and said he would send the
paper on to Mr. Russell with the amendment.

FE -- Mr. Ballantine
Subject:
Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property.
FW 894.41
With reference to your nemorandum of September 12,
1945, on t he subject of Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property, and especially to questions (A)
and (B) propounded in the memorandum appended thereto by
Mr. Straus, I would offer suggestions as follows.
With regard to (A), pending the issuance of orders
by General MacArthur to the Japanese Government, referred
to in the last paragraph under the caption "History" in
Mr. Straus' memorandum, I see no alternative to leaving
the matter in abeyance until such orders are issued. You
might wish to consider, however, sending a circular tele-
gram to all diplomatic missions to which the circular
tele ram of August 14, 8 p.m. was sent, instructing them
to desist from further action on that circular pending
action by the Supreme Allied Cormander in Japan.
With regard to (B), so far as I am aware no decision
has been reached by this Government as to whether Japan is
to have any diplomatic representation abroad. It is my
understanding that the British Government is inclined to
favor taking steps to r emove Japan's diplomatic representa-
tion abroad. It may be that there will be a decision to
that effect, but on the other hand it mayb e that we shall
ask for the recall of only certain Japanese diplomatics
abroad. In any case, it is difficult to see what action
we can take toward even t emporary custody of Japanese
archives pending action by the Suprere llied Cormander
vis-a-vis the Japanese Government.
s/s
J.W.B.

=== Page 27 === (confidence 0.98)

September 11, 1945
Subject: Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives and property.
History
On August 13, 1945, Assistant Secretary DUNN requested
Assistant ecretary HoLM S to take action regarding the
Japanese diplomatic archives and property similar to that
taken with respect to German archives and property. After
General Holmes despatched the telegrams indicated below,
the matter was turned over to FC.
On August 14, 8 pm, a circular telegram (copy attached)
was sent to All American Diplomatic Missions instructing
them to request FONoFF and protecting powers to give custody
of archives and proporty to all missions acting on behalf of
USA, USSR, UK and china.
At the same time instructions were sent to Amembassy
Bern (DEPTEL 2535, attached) requesting the Swiss to communi-
cate a note to the Japanese GoVT instructing that GOVT to
surrender custody of the property and archives. To the note
the Japanose replied that the Potsdam declaration did not
provide for such measures.
Thereupon a message was sent to eneral MacArthur
requesting him to cause the Japanese to issue appropriate
instructions with regard to this matter. The SAC replied
that he did not feel that at that time he had the necessary
authority to enforce such an order.
Shortly thereafter instructions were sent to General
lackrthur requesting that he issue such orcers when the
time was propitious.
Problem

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Problem
A) Many of our missions have taken action and have
written notes to the FONOrFS and protecting powers roquesting
custody of the archives and property. What is to be done
until meral MacArthur issues instructions - there may be 
delay of a week, a month, or a year before such instructions
are issued.
B) Is it contemplated that JAPAN is to have no representa-
tion abroad? If so, custody would be in order; if not, it
would appear that only temporary custody of archives would be
in order as the property would be needed by the Jap missions.

I have the honor to refer to the Department's
circular telegram of October 25, 8 a.m., and to its
telegran. No. 1679 of October 26, 1 p.m., and to this
Embassy's despatch No. 26,916 of 0ctober 15, 1945,
with rererence to the disposition of the Japanese
diplomatic and consular archives in Mexico.
As the Department is aware, and in accord with
its instructions 1 have been in consultation with my
British, Chinese and Soviet colleagues on this matter.
In continuation of this consultation, and in view of
the instructions above referred to, a meeting was held
on October 29 in tiis mbassy which was attended by
the British mbassador, the Chinese Chargé d'Affaires,
5
and the Russian chargé d'affaires. I explained to them
the purpose of the meeting and indicated the nature
of the instructions which I had received from the
Department. The British mbassador stated that his
instructions from the Britisi. Foreign Ofrice were
identic with mine. The Chinese Chargé d'Arraires
stated that, accordine to his instructions, he was
to follow the same procedure as that taken by this
Embassy under its instructions. The Russian Chargé
C
a'affaires did not give any inaication of the nature
S
of the instructions which he had from his Government.
/VJ
The British Ambassador, the Chinese Chargé d'Affaires
and 1 all expressed the opinion that the Mexican Govern-
ment woula not be agreeable to the Swedish Minister
turning over the Japanese diplomatic and consular
archives to the four powers, but woulu insist upon the
archives being delivered to it, as it was one of the
United Nations, and as the archives were in Mexico. We
based this opinion on the attitude of the Mexican Governt
ment in the matter of the German archives, in which case
it insisted upon the archives being taken over by it and
the contents thereof made available to the four powers.
I
n..

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-2-
I expressed the opinion that in the case of the
Japanese archives the Mexican Government would be willing
not only to make available to us the contents of the
archives, but to have access thereto. The British
Ambassador and the Rusgian Chargé were in agreement
with this opinion. chunsse
1 further expressed the opinion that the Swedish
inister here, as accredited to the mexican Government,
woula have aifriculty on the receipt of the instructions
to aeliver the archives to the four powers to do so
vithout consulting the mexican Government. I said that
the archives were in mexico; the Swedish minister was
exercising his duties here as duly accredited Minister
of the Swedish Government to the Mexican Government.
It was, as I saw it, a difficult thing for him to deliver
these archives to us, on receipt o1 instructions to that
effect from his Government, transmitted on behalf of the
Japanese Government, without informing the Foreign Office
first of the ch racter of these instructions and securing
its comment. There was agreement among all of us present
at the meetin that the swedish minister, for reasons of
procedure, would have to follow that course.
The Soviet Churgé took the position that we did not
know what the attitude of the Mexican Government would be
and that our conversation was based on assumptions. It
was obvious that he was inclined to the idea that we should
insist on the four powers taking over the archives from
the Swedish Minister. He did not say this directly, but
it was obvious from his statements.
It Was suggestea by the Soviet Chargé that I get in
touch with the Swedish Minister anu ask him whether he
had received instructions from his Government with regard
to these archives and, if so, what the nature thereof was.
I immediately did this and was informed that no instruc-
tion with regard to the Japanese archives had been re-
ceived by the Swedish Legation here.
It was therefore agreed that 1 should ask the Swedish
minister to keep us informed as soon as he got any instruc-
tions from his Government and inform us of the character
thereof, and as soon as he so informed us that we would
again meet and determine upon the procedure to be followed.
The Soviet Chargé indicated that he would not place any
dirficulties in the way of the archives being delivered
to the Mexican Government, provided the four powers had
access to them. Even in making this statement, his
language was most indirect and noncommittal, but it was
entirely clear from the tenor of his statements that he
would be willing to agree to this procedure.
The Swedish Minister has informed me that as soon as
he will receive instructions from his Government, he will
get in touch with me. It is, in my opinion, certain that
on receiving instructions the Swedish Minister will
immediately

=== Page 31 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
-3-
immediately inform the Foreign Office here. If the
Foreign Office makes no objection to the turning over of
the archives to the four powers, this will be the pro-
cedure followed. If the  oreign Office here will insist
that the archives be turned over to it by the Swedish
inister, then the representatives of the four powers
here will inform the Foreign Orfice that they are in
agreement with this procedure in this case, provided
the four powers have access to the documents. I feel
confident that the mexican Government will agree to
such direct access in the case of the Japanese archives.
I have cone into this aetail with respect to this
matter particularly for the purpose of bringing out the
fact that in the meeting on October 29, the British
Ambassador and the Chinese Chargé were most collaborative
in stating fully the attituues or their Governments and
the procedure which they were willing to follow. The
attitude of the soviet Chargé was such as to show distrust
or the Mexican Government, ana willingness to bring
pressures on it, and to make of this in reality incon-
sequential matter--as the Japanese archives were destroyed
before the Japanese representative left here--an issue
with the Mexican Goveriment. His attitude was also
curious in that he woula not indicate what his instruc-
tions were, but towards the end of the meeting stated
that once we knew what the insiructions of the Swedish
minister were, he "was sure that there would be no dif-
ficulty in finding an arrangement satisiactory all around".
I felt that the Department would be interested in
this attituue of the Soviet Chargé here, as 1 believe it
is characteristic of the procedures generally being
followed by the Soviet Government and its representatives
in so-called "collaborative" measures among the powers.
I do not anticipate any difficulty in this matter
once the Swedish Minister receives his instructions, and
1 wish to express at this time particularly my appreciation
to the Department for its telegram No. 1679 of 0ctober 26
1 p.m., which makes it possible for the archives to be
delivered to the Mexican Governent, provided we shall
have access thereto.
Respectfully yours,
George S. Messersmith

SUBJECT: Analysis of used Carbon Paper found in Japanese Legation

from Panama.

THE HONORABLE

WASHINGTON.
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch No. 2857,
October 1, 1945, transmitting a detailed inventory of whet remains of
the Japanese archives in Panama, and to report that the Federal Bur-
eau of Investigation, Washington, has completed a study of thirteen
pieces of used carbon paper that were discovered among the Japanese
Legation archives.
Seven of these thirteen pieces of carbon paper deal with the
carrying back to Japan by officers of the Legation of some $39,900
(or $33,900) which appears to be private deposits with the Yokohama
Specie Bank. A copy of the F5I report (Q1 - Q7 inclusive) on these
seven itets is attached. The remaining six pieces of carbon paper
deal with purely routine Legation matters, but copies of the FBI re-
port (Q8 - Q13 inclusive) are attached for the Department's information.
Nith regard to the Yokohama Specie Bank deposita, reference is
N
made to item No. 86 of the inventory contained with the Embassy's
despatch No. 2857, but it is submitted that item No. 86, which con-
2
sisted only of a half completed memorandum in several copies was in-
7
correctly translated on the inventory. What actually was involved was
the taking of $33,900 (or $39,900) out of Panama, not the bringing of
a sum of money to Panama.
There has not been eny branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank in Pana-
ma or the Canal Zone in the Last decade or so. Therefore, what are
described as deposits with the Yokohama Specie Bank must have been de-
posits with either the Legation or some Japaneso firm acting as informal
agent for the Yokohame Spscie Bank. The names of the individual de-
positore appear to be persons formerly in Panama (see lists contained
in inventory forwarded with despatch No. 2857), and it is therefore be-
lieved unlikely that these funds ceme from outside Panama. In eny ovent,
it appoare that comn hidden "bank" for the Japanese residents in Panama
in exicsence pigho up untal Fearl Herbor.

Panama, May 15, 1940
I wish to inform you that on the 2lst of this month there will ar-
rive at Cristobal, coming from Brazil, Mr. Junzo Sakane, Envoy Extraordinary
and Minister Plenipotentiary from Japan (named) to the Colombian Republic,
and two days before his arrival there will come to Balboa, Mrs. Sakane, and
Miss Sakane, his wife and dsughter respectively, on board the Japanese boat
"Argentina Maru," also coming from Brazil.
Minister Sakane and his family wish to remain in the city until
the 23rd of this month, the date on which they will embark on the veasel
Santa Elena, of the Grace Line for Buenaventure, Colombia.
For this reason I request you to take the necessary action in
order that the competent authorities shall grant the necessary facilities
to the aforesaid Minister and his family.
Thanking you in advance for what you shall do in this regard, with

Dear Carrigan:
I have your confidential letter of October 26,

despatch No. 26,916 of October 15, with regard to the
disposition of the Japanese archives, and I appreciate
very much the helpful information and background which
894.414/
you ive me.
You will, by this time, have received my letter
of October 31 with which I send you a copy of my
despatch No. 27,O48 of October 31, on the disposition
of the Japanese diplomatic and consular archives in
Mexico and giving the latest information in connec-
tion therewith.
You will note that on the basis of the instruc-
tions which you mention in your letter of October 26
and which had reached me, 1 had a meeting with my
British, soviet, and hinese colleagues and I am
sure that on the basis of the instructions Which I
now have, we can work out this problem here. The
important thing was for us to huve adeguate latitude
and permissive authority to let the Mexicans take
over the archives if they so wish. This we now have
and as for the rest, it is important that we have
access thereto and that 1 think we can arrange.

seem to be slow in coming throu to the wedish

Japencse Government morning of November 1:
"The Imperial Japanese Government, having decided to
take the necessary steps in compliance with the order of
the Allied Supreme Headquarters, instruct you as follows:
(My 1790, November 2 repeats this to London.)
"One. To request the Royal Swedish Government to
hand over without delay and in the present state all
Japanese diplomatic and consular properties and archives
in the country where Sweden is in charge of the protection
of Japanese interests to the respective governments of
Great Britain, Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, United
States of America, British Dominions, France and all their
colonies and dominions, and in the cases of other countries,
to the

Two. To hand over all the properties and archives
of the Japanese Legaticn and Consulate in Sweden without
delay and in the present state to the appointed representa-

After that your official relations with the Royal
Swedish Government are to cease.
Threc. To express to the Royal Swedish Government
on behalf of the Japanese Government the sincere apprecia-

Representatives of the Protocol Section of Swedish
Foreign Office and of the British, Soviet and American
Legations called at Japanese Legation 16o0 o'elock where

entrances into the Japanese Legation, including two other
separate offices which serve as annexes of the Legation.

and its annexes except for a box containing 520,000
Swedish kronor in currency which was sealed and on which
signature of each visiting representative was affixed.
The box was then delivered to the representative of the
Swedish Foreign Office for temporary safekeeping.
An inter-Allied Committee has been formed consisting
of representatives of the British, Soviet, Chinese and
American Legations, chairman of which is the American
reprcesentative. The committee is planning to meet promptly

Mr. Turner:
Fle
alaorcoran
Responsive to your chit of October 29, 1945 regarding the
Aide Memoire received from the British Embassy concerning offi-
cial and non-official records and archives in Japan, wherein
Cantrel
00/8
This Document wust Be Returmed to
you request FC's comments in regard to paragraph seven thereof,
the following is set forth:
1. The British here have apparently made their
first bid for permission to examine equally with offi-
cials of the United States such records and archives
894.414/11-545
in Japan taken over, or under the control of, United
States military authorization. Certainly it is a
trifle late to become worried about the preservation
of these archives.
2. This opens up a big question with regard to
archives in China and elsewhere in the Pacific area
since as far as I know no decision in this matter has
yet been made.
3. Undoubtedly United States Army authorities
at the time of the surrender took the necessary steps
to preserve and have access to such records.
4. Perhaps the Aide Memoire might best be kept
in a "pending" status for the present, without acknowl-
edgment.

894.414

FOREIGN ACTIVITY CORRELATION
For Mr Bradeu's
CONFIDENTIAL
November 81945
bachgroulinformatio
BA Btoe
RPA
Herewith information regarding Japanese records and archives
responsive to your chit of November 7, 1945.
The Department has been endeavoring since August 14, 1945 to
obtain custody, jointly with the other three principal allied
0
powers, of Japanese diplomatic and consular records and archives
and property (Department's CIRTEL Aug. 14, 8 p.m.). The Embassies
at London, loscow and Chungking by other telegrams of the same date
and hour were instructed to approach the Foreign Offices in those
capitals to request that appropriate instructions be issued to
their missions abroad. At the same time a telegram was sent to
Bern (2535, Aug. 14, 8 p.m.) instructing the Legation there to re-
quest the Foreign Office to communicate to the Japanese Government
a directive embodying the desires of the United States Government.
In reply to the directive communicated through the Swiss to the
Japanese the latter replied (Tel. 3929, Aug. 19, noon, from Bern)
0
that since the demands were not in accordance with the Potsdam
4
Declaration they would not comply.
5
Thereupon a message was sent to General MacArthur requesting
him to cause the Japanese to issue appropriate instructions. Since
General MacArthur felt that he would be unable to enforce such an
order he did not issue it. General lacArthur was then requested to
issue appropriate instructions at such time as he felt it, was timely
to do so.
At the same time the Department began to receive numerous
telegrams from the field raising a number of points; problems created
principally by the failure of the Japanese Government to comply with
the directive conmunicated through the Swiss. A problem was also
created by the position of the British that the Allied powers should
be satisfied with access to the archives rather than custody. This
problem arose particularly in Argentina. The British also desired
that instructions be issued to provide for the turning over of the
MR. BRADEN
archives and property in the United Kingdom, the Netherlands, France,
NOV 1 3 1945
the British Commonwealth, the Soviet Union and the United States to
PEC141945
DCR NE Unit
By circular
BF

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.99)
?79
-2-.
By circular telegram of October 13, 1945, 8 a.m., new instruc-
tions issued to General MacArthur were communicated to the field.
By circular telegram of October 23, 5 p.m. the text of the
directive to the Japanese Government from General MacArthur to be
communicated at noon, Tokyo time, October 25, was given the field.
This directive provided that physical custody of all Japanese
diplomatic and consular property and archives in the countries men-
tioned (last sentence, second paragraph above) be turned over to
the country concerned; that in countries where Sweden or Switzerland
were acting as protecting powers the archives and property should be
turned over to representatives of the four Allied Powers; that in
neutral countries likewise physical custody should be turned over to
representatives of the four powers; that routine functions of pro-
tection of Japanese nationals should continue to be handled by
Switzerland and Sweden; and that Japanese diplomatic and consular
representatives in neutral countries should be recalled.
As a result of a request from General MacArthur for information
a further circular (Cctober 25, 8 a.m.) was sent to the field advising
that the action to be taken at the various capitals to receive
Japanese records and archives was a matter for decision at each
capital in the light of local conditions and after consultation with
representatives of the other three powers.
In view of the difficulty caused in the field by the British
position to be content with access, particularly in countries of
South America, the Department sent out circular telegram of November 2,
1945, 8 a.m., stating that where serious objections to custody by the
four powers was raised by the Foreign Office the Department was pre-
pared to renounce custody in favor of access provided the eenewrrene
 the other three Allied representatives concurred in such action.
In regard to the final disposition of the Japanese diplomatic
and consular records and archives and property there has as yet been
no definite policy established. This Division feels that the impor-
tant thing is to obtain complete access--including photographic
reproduction of records--and that if certain countries desire to
assume responsibility for custody and preservation the Department
should not raise objection.
FC:PFCherp:CMM

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WESTERN
DIVISION OF/EUROPEAN AFFAIRS
November 8, 1945
FC: Mr. Cherp
The French Chargé d'Affaires, Mr. Lacoste, needled
me yesterday about their Aide Memoire of September 24, 1945
(No. 771). I subsequently located the file, which I had not
seen before, in NE and attach it herewith.
ral
You will note that the French raised two questions
ocument Wuet Be Raturned 
relating (one) to the handling of Japanese diplomatic and
consular archives in Beirut and (two) the general disposition
of Japanese public and private property situated outside Japan.
I gather that this question was originally handled by Mr. Straus
894.414/11-
and that the infant is now reposing in your lap.
As far as the first question is concerned, I personally
think that we should let the French into the picture. Although
we do not recognize a special or privileged position for France
in the Levant, there is no denying the fact that they have had
such a position in the past and that to exclude them would make
them lose face locally. I gather from Mr. Colquitt that NE would
go along with this.
As to question No. 2, which is of a more general
845
nature, I would also be inclined to include the French. I
realize, however, that this would necessitate a change in the
policy established by Assistant Secretary Holmes last August.
In any event, I think that we should try to get
some sort of decision and reply out to the French since the
matter has been hanging fire for about six weeks.
7243.
C
y
GB

Enolosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 463, dated Nov. 10, 1945.
COPY
Dal Vaticano, 1i 8 Novembre 1945.
SEGRETERIA DI STATO
DI SUA SANTITA
No. 108217.
La Secrétairerie d'Etat de Sa Santeté n'a pas
manqué de porter toute son attention sur les
dispositions des Autorités Alliées concernant les
diplomates Japonais en pays neutre (Notes n. 477,
du 31 août dernier, de M. 1'Assistant au Représentant
Personnel du Président des Etats-Unis d'Amérique
auprés du Saint-Pére, et N. 125/9/45, du 1er no-
vembre dernier, de M. le Ministre de Grande Bretagne
prés le Saint-Siège).
Le Délégué Spécial du Japon auprès du Saint-Siè
ge, en ce qui le concerne, a déclare avoir été informé
des communications faites par le Général MacArthur
au Gouvernement Japonais sur ce sujet. Il a été
informé en outre que le Gouvernement Japonais, en
réponse à ces communications, aveit demandé des
explications aux Autorités Américaines, en faisant
remarquer que ces communications ne semblaient pas
conformes aux protocolles de Potsdam.
Le Délégué Spécial du Japon n'a pas recu jusqu'i-
ci d'autres instructions ou informations de son Gouverne-
ment.
En portant ce qui précède à la connaissance de M.
l'Assistant du Representant Personnel du Président des
Etats-Unis d'Amérique auprès du Saint-Père, la Secrétairerie
d'Etat saisit l'occasion pour lui renouveler l'assurance
de sa considération bien distinguée.
M. Harold H. Tittmann
Assistant du Représentant Personnel du
Président des Etats-Unis d'Amérique
auprès du Saint-Père.

=== Page 96 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatoh No. 463, dated Nov. 10, 1945.
2
Secretariat of State
TRANSLATION
of His Holiness
No. 108217
The Secretariat of State of His Holiness did not fail to
take due note of the instructions issued by the Allied
Authorities regarding the Japanese diplomats in neutral
countries (Notes No. 477 of August 31 last from the Assistant
to the Personal Representative of the President of the
United States of America to His Holiness the Pope and
No. 125/9/45 of November 1 last from the British Minister to
the Holy See).
The Japanese Special Delegate to the Holy See, on his
part, has stated that he has been informed of General
MacArthur's communications to the Japanese Government on this
subject. He has also been informed that the Japanese Govern-
ment had, in reply to his communications, requested an
explanation from the American Authorities, drawing attention
to the fact that these communications did not appear to be
consistent with the terms of the Potsdam agreement.
Up to the present the Japanese Special Delegate has
received no further communication or instruction from his
Government.
In bringing the foregoing to the attention of the
Assistant to the Personal Representative of the President of
the United States of America to His Holiness the Pope, the
Secretariat of State takes the occasion of renewing the
assurance of his distinguished consideration.
*
*
*
*

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
3
Enclosure No. 3 to despatoh No. 463, dated November 10, 1945.
COPY
2, via Boncompagni, Rome.
No. 477
NOTE VERBALE
The Assistant to the Personal Representative of the
President of the United States of America to His Holiness
The Pope presents his compliments to the Seoretariet of State
of His Holiness and has the honor to inform the latter that the
Japanese Government upon surrender will be instructed to order
its Missions in neutral countries to relinquish to the custody
of the Allied powers all diplomatic and consular property and
archives. Pending the issuance of such Japanese instructions,
the responsible Japanese official in the Vatican City should
be given clearly to understand that any destruotion or removal
of the Archives of the Japanese Embassy to the Holy See should
be avoided. The British and Chinese Ministers to the Holy See
have indicated that they desire to be associated with the
Assistant to the Personal Representative of the President of
the United States in bringing this matter to the attention
of the Holy See.
Rome, August 31, 1945.
To the
Secretariat of State of His Holiness,
Vatican.

Enclosure No. 4 to despatch No. 463, dated November 10, 1945.
COPY
125/9/45
His Britannic Majesty's Minister to the Holy See
has the honour to invite the attention of the
Secretariat of State to the Note of the Assistant
to the Personal Representative of the President of
the United States of America to His Holiness the Pope,
No. 477 of August 3lst, on the subject of the Japanese
Mission to the Holy See.
2.
Sir D'Arcy Osborne is informed by His Majesty's
Government that Generel MacArthur on October 2lst
instruoted the Japanese Government, firstly, to turn
over intact and without delay the physical custody
of all Japanese diplomatic and consular property
and archives to the Representatives of the Allied
Powers designated to receive them, and, secondly,
immediately to recall Japanese diplomatic and consular
Representatives in neutral countries.
3。
His Britannic Majesty's Minister would be grate-
ful if the Secretariat of State would inform him whether
the Japanese Representative has yet received these
instructions.
British Legation to the Holy See,
R O M E.
November 1st 1945.

subjects:

llowever, as the note does not inuicate whether or not the

to obtain direct passage from Ireland to Japan, it. is

to those who are ordered home.

=== Page 133 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
Enclosure no. 2 to Despatoh no. 13i 7dated
Nowember 4, 1945, from the Amerioan
Embassy at Buenos Aires, Argentina.
NOTE TO SWISS LEGATION
The Embassy of the United States of America
presents its compliments to the Swiss Legation and
has the honor to acknowledge receipt of oral notice
that the Swiss Legation has been instructed to
relinquish custody immediately of former Japanese
diplomatic and consular property and archives in
Argentina to representatives of the four Allied
Powers.
In this connection representatives in Argentina
of the Embassies of two of these Powers, namely the
United States of America and Great Britain, will
arrange with the Swiss Legation to take immediate
possession of Japanese property and archives on
behalf of the interested Powers.
It is understood that the Swiss Legation has
also been instructed to continue to handle routine
functions for the protection of Japanese nationals,
including the affairs of the diplomatic internees.
Buenos Aires, November 10, 1945.
GW: jad:v1

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
Enclosure no. 3 to Despateh no. 131t dated
November , 1945, from the Ameriean
Embassy at Buenos Aires, Argentima.
NOTE VERBALE
The Embassy of the United States of Ameriea
presents its compliments to the Ministry of Foreign
Affairs and Worship and has the honor to state that
in acoordance with instructions from the Japanese
Government the Swiss Legation in Buenos Aires today
relinquished its custody of all former Japanese
diplomatic and consular property and arohives in
the Argentine Republic to the Embassies of the United
States of America and Great Britain, acting on behalf
of the four Allied Powers, namely the Republic of
China, the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics, the
United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland,
and the United States of America.
The Swiss Legation will however continue its
routine functions on behalf of Japanese nationals,
including Japanese diplomatic internees.
The Embassy will be pleased to grant the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs access to the Japanese archives in
accordance with the provisions for exchenge of informa-
tion contemplated in Resolution LII of the Inter-
American Conference on Problems of War and Peace.
Buenos Aires, November 13, 1945.

greater part of which is in storage, only a minor part being
in use, will ve left in the Chancery of the Swedish Legation
until July 1, 1936, on which date the Swedish Minister's
lease on his Chancery expires: a considerable amount of
furniture and such household effects as silverware, linen,
pictures, etc.; certain belongings of the Japanese Military

reference is made to the Department's memorandum dated

1.0116

quested concerning the disposition and use of the archives.

sent to the United States by one of the United States Army

I recommend that these archives be sent to Washington, since
the United States Government, as representative of the
principal Allied Powers, has obtained outright custody of
the archives and since there are no local facilities for
translating the archives.

Enclosure No. 1
COPY
Despatch No. 13,047
Santiago, Chile
November 16, 1945
LEGATION DE SUEDE
Santiago, November 5th, 1945.
Mr. Ambassador,
The Royal Swedish Minister of Foreign Affairs has
informed me that the Japanese Minister in Stockholm, in
behalf of is Government, has requested my Government
to direct me to hand over to a representative of the
Allied Powers all the Japanese diplomatic and consular
property, wnich was left in my custody by the former
Japanese Minister to Chile.
To that end, I have been authorized to deliver this
property to the person that may be selected jointly by
you, Mr. Ambassador, the British Ambassador and the
Chinese Chargé d'Affaires.
Consequently, I have the honour to ask you to be
rood enough to let me know if you desire to take over
the aforementioned Japanese Government property in trust
for the Allied Powers. In the affirmative, I should be
much obliged if you, together with your British and
Chinese Colleagues would appoint a representative, who
will be authorized to receive that property for and on
behalf of the Allied Powers.
At the same time I beg to inform you that the
Japanese Government has requested my Government that
this Legation shall continue to handle ordinary routine
work concerning the protection of Japanese citizens in
Chile, provided that the representatives of the main
Allied Powers have no objections.
With renewed assurances of my highest considera-
tion, I have the honour to remain,
Sir,
Your most obedient servant
(Signed) F. Wennerberg
His Excellency
Mr. Claude G. Bowers
American Ambassador
etc. etc. etc.

Santiago, November 5, 1945.
My dear Mr. Minister:
I have received your letter of November 5, 1945,

Foreign Affairs has informed you that the Japanese
Minister in Stockholm, in behalf of his Government,
has requested your rovernment to direct you to hand
over to a representative of the Allied Powers all the

left in your custody by the former Japanese Minister
to Chile.
My British and Chinese colleagues, in the absence
of a Russian diplomatic official in Chile, have author-
ized me to act as their representative in receiving the
Japanese Government property above mentioned for and
on behalf of the Allied Powers. Therefore, I desire
to take over the Japanese Government property concerned
at such time as may be convenient to you.
I have no objection to the continuance by the
Swedish Legation of the handling of ordinary, routine
work concerning the protection of Japanese subjects
in Chile.

in reply of Mir. Claude G. Bowers, Ambassador of the United
States of Anerica, dated November 5, 1945, concerning the de-
livery to a representative of the Allied Powers of all the
Japanese diplomatic and consular property which was left in
the custody of the Swedish inister by the former Japanese
inister to Chile.
The British Anbassador and the Chinese Chargé d'Affaires
have by their letters dated November 6, 1945, authorized
the American Ambassador to act as their representative in
receiving the Japanese Government property above mentioned
for and on behalf of the Allied Fowers.
The Swedish Minister handed over today, November 6, 1945,
to the American Ambassador an inventory of the Japanese
diplomatic and consular property, including archives, formerly
in the possession of the Japanese  inister and also the follow-
ing items:
1. Special box o. 7 containing domuments
(See page 12 of the inventory);
A bound volume containing a list of the
Japanese subjects in Chile, with pertinent
data;

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
3. Chilean bonds with a par value of
3,000,000(Chilean);
4. 900,000 (Chilean) in cash
The remainder of the cash left by the Japanese Minister
with the Swedish Minister, to the value of 304,544.18 (Chilean),
is being left in the possession of the Swedish Legation for
expenditures in connection with the routine protection of Japanese
interests in Chile. (See attached statement and copy of letters
exchanged on arch 10, 1943, by the Japanese Minister and
the Swedish Chargé a'affaires, together with a copy of two
enclosures).
The Swedish Legation is willint to deliver the remainder
of the Japanese diplomatic and consular property, which is
now in the Chancery of the Swedish Legation, when mutually
convenient to that Legation and the American Embassy.

Folke Wennerberg,
Claude G. Bowers
Swedish Minister
American Ambassador

I.- Banco Central de Chile
150 bank notes in the amount of ten thousands (10,000.-)
pesos each in the total sun of one million five hundred
thousand (1,500,000.-) 0hilean pesos.
II.

each in the total sun of seven hundred thousand (70o,o00.-
Chilean pesos.
B. 70 bonds of Banco Hipotecaria, Valparafso, Serie 6%-1%, a
10,ooo.-pesos each in the total sum of seven hundred thou-
sand (700,000.-) Chilean pesos.
C. 16O bonds República de Chile Deuda Interna, Serie Z - II
7%-1%, a 10,o00.- each in the total sum of one million six
hundred thousand (1,00,000.-) Chilean pesos.
Santiago de Chile, March 10th. 1943
Kiyoshi Yamagata

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's circu-
894.414/11
lar telegram of November 16, 9 a.m., 1945, stating that
the Department desires a mail report on procedure followed
in taking over Japanese diplomatic and consular records,
property and archives. In this connection there are en-
closed herewith a memorandum of a meeting held in the Em-
bassy on November 5, 1945, and a memorandum of a meeting
held in the office of the British Ambassador on November 7,
1945, since which no material progress has been made in
respect to the delivery of the Japanese archives to the
-1945
representatives of the four powers.
Upon inquiry, the British Ambassador informed the Em-
bassy today that up to the present time no reply has been
received by the Swedish Chargé d'Affaires to his note
addressed to the Mexican Government in the premises. The
British Ambassador today urged the Swedish Chargé d'Affaires
to press the Mexican Foreign Office for a reply to his note.
As soon as there are any further developments in this
matter the Department will be informed.
Respectfully yours,
CS
Raymond H. Geist,
Chargé d'Affaires, ad interim

Mexico, D. F.
November 5, 1945
MEMORANDUM
Mr. Petersen, Chargé d'Affaires of the Swedish Lega-
tion, called this morning at the Embassy, and informed
chat he had received instructions from his Government to
turn over the Japanese property, funds and archives to
the representatives in exico ity of the United States
of America, Great Lritain, China and the Soviet Socialist
Republics. he said tat e came to give ne this informa-
tion in response to a request from the United States
Ambassador, who left this morning by airplane for Washing-
ton.
Mr. Fetersen stated thet he intended to inform the
Mexican Foreign Office of the substance of his instruc-
tion, and was in doubt as to whether or not he should in-
form the Mexican Foreign Office at the same time that he
had sdvised the Chargé a'Affaires of the United States on
behalf of the four powers of the substance of his
instructions. He thought that when he advised the Mexican
Foreign Office of these instructions they might take
offense at his having given advice in the premises to the
representatives of the four powers before he had spoken
with the Mexican Foreign Office. Mr. Petersen was in
doubt as to the wisdor of this procedure and esked my
advice. I informed him t!at at the last meeting of the
representatives of the four powers it was decided to seek
information from tre Swedish Legation as to the contents
 the instruetions when they were received; and since he
had now civen notice that the instructions had been re-
ceived, and the purpors of tose instructions, it would
be incumbent upon me to aivise the British Ambassador so
that he could cail a meeting at his office where the
question and :rocedure vould be further discussed and de-
cided upon. I stated that I thought if, in view of these
circumstances, he feareu to advise the Mexican authorities
that he had informed te representatives of the four
powers of the receipt of his instructions, an embarrassing
situation might be created if the Foreign Office requested
him to withhold further action in the matter until they
nad an opportunity to consider it. Furthermore, I observed
that it would be necessery for the Foreign Office to know
that the representatives of the four powers had been
advised, if in the meantime it should be decided by the
representatives of the four powers to approach the
Foreign Office in the premises.

according to MacArthur's instructions, the archives were to

The British Ambassador ventured the suggestion that we
four colleagues could take no action in the premises before
the Mexican Government replied to the Swedish note. In
this respect it was explained that the Swedish Legation had
charge of Japanese property, funds, archives and nationals
only throuth thhe consent of the Mexican authorities. The
Russian Chergé d'Affaires asked, in this connection, if the
Swedish Chargé's instructions indicated that he was to
communicate on the matter with the exican Foreign Office.
I assured him that the instructions were not to that effect.
The British Anbassador said that "there was such a thing as
international law and procedure, and that the Swedish
Jhargé was obligeo to notify the Mexicen authorities first
of all with respect to his instructions". It was agreed
among us al. that the swedish Jhargé was correct in approach-
ing the Mexican Foreign Office, which would have to decide,
before any rurt er action could be taien by the four
colleagues, whether any objections existed on the part of
the Mexicen Foreign Office to turn the archives over to the
representatives of the four powers.
At first, the Russian Chargé seemed not to be in
agreement with this rocedure; and indicated that his
covernment would expect him, in conformity with the terms
of MacArtlur's instructions, to consummate the negotiations
by obtaining possession of the archives.

could make no movement intil we now what the attitude of
the Mexican Government is, the Russian Chargé d'Affaires
expressed himself in agreement with this procedure.

adopt if the Mexican Foreign Office had no objections to
the urocedure f the Swedish Jhargé d'Affaires in turning

attitude o? the mexican authorities was opposed to the
delivery of the arcrives to the representatives of the
four powers.
After it was unanimously decided to await the reply
to the Swedish Chargé's note to the Foreign Office, the
meeting adjourned, sine die.

1945 concerning disposition of property belonging to former
Japanese officials in Germany who were brought to the United

the automobiles mentioned in your telegram no. 358 of October 19,
414/11-2145
1945 are not in condition to use. Inform Department when the

property shipped to the United States for disposition. This
instruction should be considered as superseding instructions sent
by War Department to Paris, Commanding General, USFET, Rear in

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
ww
I have the honor to acknowledge the recelpt
Department's circular telegram of November 16, 1945,
requesting the submission of a report by mail on pro-
cedure followed with reference to Japanese diplomatic
XN 701.9455
and consular records, archives and property as well as
02.9455
a brief description of such records and property, and
in immediate compliance with the Department's wishes
I have the honor to make the following report:
89
As I reported to the Department in my despatch
No. 26v of August 28, 1945, immediately upon the rocoipt
4
of the Department's circular telegram of August l4th
relative to Japanese archives, I went to the Foreign
Office and expressed the Department's desires in this
connection. This visit was followed by a note setting
forth the Department's wishes. The Grand Ducal
Ministry of Foreign Affairs immediately took action
in securing all Japanese property and archives in
this country, and some days later sent me a note in-
forming me that the former Honorary Consul General of
Japan, Mr. J. P. ARENDT, had turned over to the
Foreign Office what is believed to be all Japanese
5
property within this country.
The Grand Ducal Government, which is a founding
member of the United Nations on the same basis and with
much longer standing than either France or the Union
of Soviet Socialist Republics, has thus far retained
its custody of these Japanese arohives in the same
way in which the Department instructed that France,
the Netherlands, the United Kingdom, China, the United
States, the British Commonwealth and the Union of
Soviet Socialist Republics should carry out their
responsibilities: i.e. these archives have been
receipted for by the Foreign Office, placed under
seal, and are still in the Foreign Office.
The Japanese archives consist as the Department
has already been informed by me of 17 dossiers of
11.2845
general
15

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
general commeroial correspondence from 1925 to 1940,
a price list of metallurgical products comprising one
dossier; one dossier of commercial reports from Japan,
two dossiers containing consular reports from 1925 to
1940, two rubber stamps, one in German and one in
French, and one shield. For the Department's con-
fidential information I feel that the Grand Ducal
Government as an Allied power and full fledged member
of the United Nations would not willingly relinquish
these archives, solely as a matter of principle,
connected with its own sovereignty. On the other
hand there is no question of my access to these
archives for further inspection or examination should
the Department still have interest in them.
Respectfully yours,
George Platt Waller
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim

SUBJECT:
German-Japanese Archives and Property in

The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
C
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's two circular
telegrams of November 16, 8 a.m. and 9 a.m. respectively, on the
above subject, and to report that the property and archives of
九R
the former German Consulate at Wellington were taken into custody
by the New Zealand Ministry of External Affairs at t he end of
7024444H
July, 1945. Liost of the property (mainly office furniture) was
sold at public auction and the monies credited to the Custodian
of Enemy Property. The archives, packed into 32 German-sealed
cases and one safe, are stored in the vaults of Parliament Build-
ings at Wellington. There is no list of the contents of these
cases and, since it has not been possible to s pare a qualified
officer to undertake the task of examining the archives, the
894.414/11
cases have not been opened by the Ministry of External Affairs.
The Japanese property and archives will shortly be trans-
ferred from the Swiss Consul to the Ministry of External Affairs
and will be dealt with in the same way as the German.
The Ministry of External Affairs will be pleased to give
every assistance should the State Department wish to have anyone
examine these archives.
Respectfully yours,
6
45
Prescott Childs,

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Reference is made to steps recently taken outstde
the United States in conformity with the Allied directive
of October 25, 1945 to the Japanese Government concerning
the disposition of Japanese diplomatic and consular
premises, property and archives, including the private
Files
Centrel
UC/8
s Document Must Be Returned to
property of Japanese diplomatic and consular officers.
701.
a4
Numerous telegrams which have recently come into the
Department contain inquiries regarding the disposition of
707
such items as furniture, household effects, automobiles
894.414/12
etc. In order that a uniform reply may be made to the
missions concerned, it is deemed desirable that a general
policy should be established with regard to the disposition
of such effects. For such a statement of policy SPD
suggests the following:
"Official Japanese property should be stored
except when items are present in excessive quantities
2-
or when storage is impracticable in view of extreme
space requirements, depreciation or deterioration".
545
Such a statement as the foregoing sets forth a policy
consistent with that adopted in connection with similar
5
German property. Furthermore, where an extreme position of
liquidating all German diplomatic and consular property
might have been adopted in view of the absence of a German
Government it is noted that there is a Japanese Government
and that the Allied directive of October 25 provided for
894.414
transfer of the "physical custody of all Japanese diplomatic
and consular property and archives". Thus, although special
circumstances related to storage might in some instances
indicate that sale of the property and the depositing of the
proceeds of such sale to an appropriate account be necessary
the general endeavor should be to refrain from sale and
complete liguidation if the conditions set forth in the above
statement of policy can be met.
SPD would appreciate receiving the views of Le, JK, JA 
and FE with respect to the foregoing.
5
1104
SPD:REHibbard:MA
1-9-46

=== Page 256 === (confidence 0.98)
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
No. 174
CONFIDENTIAL
Subject:
: Transfer of Archives and Property of Japanese Legation.
THE HONORABLE
THE SEORETARY OF STATE,
SEPARTMENT OF
WASHINGTON.
LIAISON OFFICE
SIR:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's Circular
telegram of November 16. 9 a.m., directing that a report be
submitted concerning the procedure followed in taking over the
894.414/12-545
archives and property of the Japanese Legation in Tehran.
On receipt of instructions a meeting was held on November
10 by representatives of the four Allied Powers, China. Great
Britain. the Soviet Union and the United States, and the
representative of the Protecting Power, Sweden. At that time
it was ascertained that the Japanese property and archives were
being stored by the Swedish Legation in the rented basement of
a private house. It was agreed by representatives of the four
Allied Powers that continued storage in this manner was both
unnecessary and undesirable from the point of view of security
and the complex accounting which such a method multilaterally
would entail. Furthermore, since there was ample space in the
basement of the German Legation, now in the custody of the four
Allied Powers and Iran, it was decided unanimously that the
Japanese property and archives should be stored there.
(Reference Embassy's telegram 937, November 10 and Departme nt's
telegram 658, November 29.)
Accordingly, the British representative was delegated to
A
make informal arrangements to this end with the Iranian
Government. These arrangements effected, the property and
CS/VJ FILED
archives were physically transferred under the supervision of
representatives of the four Allied Powers and the Protecting
Power, and on November 30. 1945 the seals of the diplomatic
Missions of the four Allied Powers and of the Imperial Ministry
of For eign Affairs of Iran, were affixed to the door of the
SEP 29 1947
basement room where they are stored. On the same day a
protocol (signed copy enclosed) was signed and sealed. the
original being given to the Charge d'Affaires of the Swedish
Legation, and a signed copy to the representative of each of
the four Allied Powers. Annexed to the protocol was an
inventory of the property and archives (3 dopies enclosed).
The Embassy
2
CONFIDENTÍAL

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
The Embassy has received a communication dated November 5.
1945 from Colonel Harry S. Aldrich, Commanding, Strategic
Services Unit, Near East Theater of Operations. Cairo, requesting
that arrangements be made to allow a representative of his
office to examine the Japanese records for matters of military.
espionage and general intelligence nature. The Embassy in its
telegram No. 962. November 20, requested the Departme nt's
instructions on the matter, suggesting that local examination
was inadvisable on the grounds of lack of suitable space, and
of the probability of arousing other Powers, partioularly the
Russians, who might wish to be present at any examination,
thereby occasioning delays and possible difficulties. The
Embassy suggested alternatively that the archives be eventually
shipped to some central place for examination and coordination
with other Japanese documents. The Department's telegram 658.
November 29, implies that arrangements for examination are now
being made. The Embassy presumes that it will be duly informed
on the completion thereof.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Imant
Robert Rossow, Jr.,
Third Secretary of Embasay

As this matter has developed, the directions

apparent aims of the British Foreign Office as in-

appear to emphasize slightly divergent methods of
aohieving the results desired.
I see no reason to think that any suoh divergencies

On the matter of arohives: A ohange from the idea

Since that date there have bee: a number of meetinrs of representa-
tives of the Embassies of China, Great Britein and the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republics, meeting in the American Lmbassy. As a result
of these conversations, individual notes were sent by the four Em-

While the Foreign Office in its aclhnowledpment of the receipt of
7
these commmunications did not express any views on the subject of the
proposed transfer, oral advices fro. the Chief or the Political Section
of the Foreign Office have been te the effect that the Belgian Govern-
SEP
ment did not intend to contest the substitution of the four Embassies
for the Swedish Legation as custodie. of Japanese official property
291947
and archives in this country.
While this proved disappointing to the British who had, as a
matter of convenience, hoped that the Belgians would voice a desire
to assume custody of Japanese interests themselves inasmuchas Belgium
has been at least technically a belligerent vis-a-vis Japan, it was
decided to proceed at once to tale steps formally to take over the
894.414
premises of the Japanese Embassy, No. 1, Boulevard General Jacques,
Brussels. The Swedish Legation has described these premises as con-
taining all the known diplomatic and consular archives and property
of the Japanese Government in Belgium, the contents of the Japanese
Consulate

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.98)

Consulate at Antwerp which have been housed in a leased building in

Accordingly, represe:tatives of the four Missions, together with

the morning of December 5, 1945. We were accompanied by a Belgian
inventory expert whom I proposed to my colleagues and who had been
accepted by then. as a technician able to provide competent advice and
who is qualified to direct the worlk of taking an inventory. However,
each l ission, that of the Soviet in particular, indicated a desire to
take an independent record of the contents of the premises so a member
of the staff of each Embassy was assiened to this work which, due to
the size of the premises and the varied nature of the contents, may
take a fortnight to complete. The detailed work of inventory taking
began on the morning of December 6. The offices of the l'ilitary and
Naval Attachés to this Embassy have indicated that the War and Navy
Departments were interested in the Japanese archives end were duly
informed of the sters taken.

building, a Belgian who has been retained by the Swedish Legation and
who seems to be a responsible man who has looked after the property
for some years, will be acceltable to the four hissions should their
Goverments approve.

Embassy in the Japanese Embassy building has been lively. The Soviet

have indicated that their lission is handicapped for lack of a suit-
able chancery (the Embassy offices are at present in the Palace Hotel

to rent or buy tho house and lot for its own use and even went so
far as to supgest that the four Embassies or their Governments might
find it convenient to distribute the contents of the house among them-
selves. While this somewhat predatory proposalwas indeed tempting in
view of the discovery of a quantity of vintagesor repute in the Embassy
basement,

as reported in my telegran no. 233 or December 5, the
Jupanese Consul General continues to exnibit an unrealistic
attitude not in keepine with the fact that his country has
signed an instrument of unconditional surrender which calls
for compliance witn instructions of the Supreme Allied
Commanuer in Tokyo and nis representatives. The Japanese
Consul General appears to be anxious to leave Dublin and
return to Tokyo. He aoes not appear, however, to be will-
ing to deviate from his position of claiming equality with
our Consul General and the British Trade Commissioner.
CS/VJ
The British Representative in Dublin agrees with the
Legation that the Japanese Consul General should be in-
structed to comply fully with our wishes. We believe that
our demands are not unjust and that the data we asked for
in our memorandun of November 10, 194 and repeated
December 4 should be furnished to us so that we can
properly decide vwhether the archives and property which
he wishes to turn over to us really represent the official
archives and property. Ne do not feel that it is for him
to dictate to us. It is obvious that we should be allowed
to use our discretion in formulating our demands. The
Department's

attitude, even thougn it may mean a certain loss of face
to him.

Subject:
With reference to the disposition of the

出
The Honoratle
894.414
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. c.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to previous correspondence
between the Department and this Enbassy with regard to the
disposition of the Japanese archives and roperty in Mexico.
In these despatches and letters the action of the Embassy
in this respeet has been reported.
In accord with the Department's instructions to
collaborate with my British, Soviet and Chinese colleagues
C7
in this matter, the Department is aware that I have been in
constant touch with them. On my return from Washington
recently, I informed myself with regard to the stetus of
this matter and found that the Swedish Minister here, who
has been acting for Jupanese interests, had addressed a
note to the Mexican Foreign Office and had received a reply.
I therefore arranged for a meeting in this Embassy of the
representatives of the Governments interested, and the
British Ambassador was present at the meeting held on
December 4. Although the Soviet Ambassador had already
presented his credentials, he sent as his representative
the Soviet First Secretary. The Chinese Chargé d'Affaires
was present elso.
22
There is appended hereto (enclosure 1) a memorandum
19
made by the Counselor of this Embassy, Mr. Geist, of this
meeting, from which it will be noted that the representative
of the Soviet Emoassy took a very difficult and an eltogether
inexplicable attitude. It was impossible for my British and
Chinese

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
AIR MAIL
2
Chinese colleagues and for me to determine what was
behind the difficult attitude assumed by the Soviet
representative.
In view of the fact that the British Ambassador
had received an instruction from his Government, which
I had not received from my Government, and which apparent-
ly my Soviet colleague had not received, and which the
Chinese Chargé d'Affaires stated he also had not received,
this instruction was discussed, as will be shown by the
appended memorandum (enclosure 1). In view thereof, I
asked the British Ambassador to be good enough to send
me the substance of the instruction which he had received,
which he did in a letter dated December 5, and of which a
copy is transmitted herewith (enclosure 2).
It was agreed at the meeting on December 4 that I
should address a letter to the Swedish Minister in a
form then decided upon, and I am sending herewith a copy
of the letter addressed to the Swedish Minister, and
which is self-explanatory (enclosure 3).
I an also sending herewith a copy of the note which
the Swedish Minister addressed to the Mexican Foreign
Office (enclosure 4), as well as a copy of the reply
which the Swedish Minister received from the Foreign
Office (enclosure 5). It should be noted in this con-
nection that I was informed in the Mexican Foreign
Office that its note to the Swedish Minister was an
indication that it had no objection to the archives
being turned over to the Four Powers. Whether this
would also involve no objection on the part of the
Mexican Government to turning over cash and property,
or the proceeds from the sale of property, to the Four
Powers, I am not able to state, but it would appear
from the reply of the Foreign Office to the Swedish
Minister that it would have no objection.
It was agreed at the meeting on December 4 that we
would await the reply from the Swedish Minister in which
would be given the information requested in my letter to
the Swedish Hinister of December 5, a copy of which is
hereto appended (enclosure 3).
It was further agreed that after we have received
the reply of the Swedish Minister we will have a further
meeting.
In view of the instruetion which the British

+he substance of which is  iven in the Ambassador's

Ambassador has received is based upon conversations
between our Government and the British Government,
and if cur Government is in accord with the procedure
outlined in the instruction which the British Ambassador
has received. It will be helpful to me in following
this matter and in getting action thereon to have a
reply
