AIR MAIL
No. 1
(Enclosure/to Despatch No. 27,64l of December 10, 1945)
MEMORANDUM
Mexioo, D. F.,
December 4, 1945
A meeting was held today at the American Embassy, at
which were present the American and British Ambassadors,
the Chinese Chargé d'Affaires, and Mr. Yakubovski, Counselor
of the Russian Embassy, who represented the Russian Ambassa-
dor, to discuss further the disposition of Japanese archives
and property.
Mr. Messersmith said that he had seen Lic. Campos Ortiz,
of the Mexican Foreign Office, regarding a number of matters,
and during the conversation had incidentally inquired about
the note which the Swedish Chargé d'Affaires had sent to the
Foreign Office regarding his instructions to turn over Japa-
nese archives and property to the representatives of the four
powers. Lic. Campos Ortiz had informed the Ambassador that
the Mexican Government had received the note and had sent a
reply taking cognizance of the contents of the Swedish note,
implying that the Mexicans were not opposed to turning over
the archives and property to the four powers, noting a dif-
ference between the cases of the German and Japanese archives,
being opposed to turning over the German archives as no
German Government exists while in the case of the Japanese,
there is a Japanese Government, hence the Mexican Government
saw no objection to having the directive from Tokyo via
Stockholm carried out.
Mr. Messersmith then informed that he had got in touch
with the Swedish Minister and learned that the Swedish
Minister had received a reply from the Foreign Office re-
garding the Japanese archives and agreed to send a copy of
the note addressed to the Foreign Office and a copy of the
Foreign Office's reply. Having these notes in his possession,
Mr. Messersmith read the text of the notes to the other
representatives.
The British Ambassador thereupon observed that he had
kept in touch with the Swedish Legation and was surprised,
in view of their promise, that they had given him no informa-
tion with regard to the receipt of the note. Though the
reply was dated the 16th of November, it was thought a
considerable delay may have ensued before it reached the
Swedish Legation.
The British Ambassador then informed that he had re-
ceived in the meantime instructions from his government
with/

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL.
- 2 -
with regard to the disposition of the Japanese archives,
and read the contents of the instructions.
The instructions to the British Ambassador suggested
the expediency of sending the Japanese archives to Washing-
ton. Mr. Messersmith, who had recently been in Washington,
gave some background on this subject and explained that
provisions had been made in Washington for housing, catalogu-
ing, indexing and generally making availeble systematically
the data contained; so that the interested governments
could have their representatives consult the papers with
ease and facility. This plan appeared to have the approval
of the British and Chinese representatives. The Russian
representative felt that it was necessary that there be
made available to the representative of each of the powers
a detailed statement of what existed, and insisted that
this detailed statement should be sent to each one directly
by the Swedish Legation. The American Ambassador believed
that the proper procedure would be for him to write a letter
to the Swedish Minister, asking for a complete list of the
property. The Russian was agreeable to this, but made a
special point of the necessity of having the communication
from the Swedish Minister sent directly to each. This was
commonly agreed upon. Mr. Messersmith then suggested that
the Swedish Minister be requested to give specific informa-
tion on the following points:
1. Archives - where these were located and what
they consisted of.
2. Immovable property, if any, and details con-
cerning such property.
3. Movable property - where located and enumera-
tion of the items, etc.
4. What funds existed; also accounts as well as
current obligations.
5. Leases on diplomatic and consular property,
dates, amounts involved, expiration,
conditions, etc.
At this point the Russian representative wanted to
know if, in addressing this communication to the Swedish
Minister, the American Ambassador would make any mention
of the note which the Swedish Minister received from the
Foreign Office. He called attention to the fact that he
had received no copy of the note. Mr. Messersmith
observed that he had the copy before him and had read the
contents of it to the other representatives present. The
Russian again repeated that he had no copy.  The Russian
then wanted to know if it was necessary to send the letter
to the Swedish Minister in the name of all of the
representatives/

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.98)
AIR MAIL
- 3 -
representatives; as he appeared to be opposed t.. having
the American Ambassador conduct the correspondence on be-
half of all. Mr. Messersmith stated generally in what form
he proposed to address the Swedish Minister, with which
statement the Russian representative appeared not to be
satisfied, and stated that he felt that he must study the
note from the Mexican Foreign Office to the Swedish
Minister before he could be satisfied with the suggested
procedure. The British Ambassador at this point said,
"Well, study it now." The American Ambassador asked the
Russian what was it that he wanted. The Russian said he
wanted a copy, whereupon the American Ambassador, having
three extra copies of each note, made distribution of copies
of the notes to the Russian, British and Chinese representa-
tives. The Russien then asked if the Swedish Minister said
nothing about giving a copy of the note to the others, and
Mr. Messersmith, in reply to that observation, repeated
once more most of what he had already said, particularly
how he had got in touch informally with the wedish
Minister and under what circumstances the Swsdish Minister
had sent copies of the notes. The Russian emed not to be
satisfied with what had transpired and with she proposed
procedure. At this point both the American and British
Ambassadors made it clear to the Russian representative
that they saw no reason for splitting hairs over the precise
wording of the letter which the American Ambassador pro-
posed to send to the Swedish Minister asking for full de-
tails with regard to the Japanese archives and property,
whereupon Mr. Messersmith repeated again the text he had in
mind of the letter he proposed to send to the Swedish
Minister. The Russian, however, appeared not to be wholly
satisfied with what had taken place and with what had been
proposed, regarding which he eventually became satisfied,
namely: he appeared not to want the Ambassador's letter to
the Swedish Minister to contain any acknowledgment or men-
tion of the note addressed by the Foreign Office to the
Swedish Legation. He was in agreement with the proposed
statement in the Ambassador's letter to the Swedish
Minister that copies of the notes exchanged between the
Swedish Legation and the Mexican Foreign Office had been
given to the representatives of the other three powers.
He was also in agreement with the proposed request to be
included in the Ambassador's letter to the effect that
copies of the detailed reply with regard to Japanese
archives and property should be sent directly to the re-
presentatives of the four powers.
After agreements had been reached on these points, it
was proposed and accepted that the four representatives
meet again after the Swedish Minister had furnished all
the representatives with a complete statement with regard
to Japanese archives, property, etc.
a neaed  ecel
Raymond H. Geist

Enclosure 2 to despatch No. 27,641 of
AIR MAIL
December 10, 1945, from the Embassy at
México City.
7
C O E Y
British Embassy,
CONFIDENTIAL
Mexico City,
December 5, 1945.
My dear Colleague,
With reference to our conversation of December 4th,
I have the pleasure to transcribe below the latest proposals
of my Government regarding the disposal of Japanese diplomatic
and consular archives and property, subject to the concurrence
of yourself and the representatives of China and the U.S.S.R.:-
Archives
While leaving the initiative to you, I am to agree that
the archives should be transported to Washington, on condition
that any representative appointed by the Foreign Office or of
the British Security Co-ordination should, at all times, have
free access to the documents in Washington.
Immovable Official Property
Where diplomatic or consular buildings or offices are
rented, the lease should be terminated as soon as practicable.
Where the puildings are the property of the Japanese Government,
they should be sold or leased, depending upon local conditions.
The proceeds should be paid into an account to be administered,
pending its aisposal, by the representatives of the Four Powers.
Other Property
All official furniture ana other movable property, other
than erchives, stamps ana seals, should be sold, the proceeds
being paid into the account mentioned above. Stamps and seals
should be treated us archives.
Protection of Japanese Nationals
By agreement between the representatives of the Four
Powers, the representative of the protecting power may retain,
from existing Japanese funds or those realised by the sale or
lease of property, such amounts as may be necessary for the
continued protection of Japanese nationals.
2. So far as I can see, there are no local conditions which
would render the above proposals impracticable. We have, of
course, no reel terms of reference for dealing with these
matters and my instructions speak continually of "The concur-
rence of colleagues etc.". Presumably, this means that we
must secure unanimity at every step, but I personally, would
prefer to proceed by majority vote.
3. We are likely to have difriculty over the Japanese
money now held by the Swedish Minister and any possible
proceeds
His Excellency
George S. Messersmith,
Ambassador of the United States of America,
México, D. F.

=== Page 309 === (confidence 1.00)
AIR MAIL
-2-
proceeds of sales of property. My own view is that it
would be preferable, at least until the Japs in this part
of the worla are repatriated, for the Swede to retain such
funds for "protection" purposes. This would seem to be in
accordance with the proposals of my Government set out
above.
Believe me,
Yours very sincerely,
C. H. Bateman (signed)

BY COURIER
EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Buenos Aires, Argentina, December M, 1945
Central Information Orrice
(C.I.O.) Report.
JK
No.
1557
Zile
Subject:
A Cursory Examination of Former Japanese
Diplomatic and Gensular Arehives in Argentina.
3-5=46
SECRET
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
no. 1317 dated November 14, 1945, entitled "Transfer by
894.414/12-1245
Swiss Legation of Former Japanese Diplomatic and Consular
Property and Arohives in Argentina to Allied Control",
and to previous telegrams on the subjeot of assumption
of custody from the Swiss Legation of such property and
archives by the American and British Embassies on behalf
of the four Allied Powers.
In this connection are enclosed 17 memoranda regard-
ing a cursory examination of the archives acquired from
the Swiss Legation, as made by Second Secretary Gerald
Warner of this Embassy over a period of two weeks before
his departure on transfer.
1-12/
Enclosures nos. 1-12 list the contents of 12 wooden
boxes containing mainly books used by the former Japanese
Embassy and Consulate. Box no. 12 contains several con-
sular records of possible interest.
13/
Enclosure no. 13 lists the contents of a steel
cabinet which contained numerous confidential Japanese
Embassy and Consular records for the years l932, and
1936-l943 inclusive. A registry of Japanese in Argentina
listed as being inoluded in this cabinet was not found
when the oabinet was opened.
14-16/
Enclosures nos. 14, 15, and 16 list the contents of
three trunks given to the Swiss Legation by the Japanese
under seal for custody. These three trunks contain the
records of most interest of all those examined, and the
Department might wish to have these records sent to
Washington for further detailed study. The British Em-
bassy has no objection to the despatch of these records
for this purpose. The records of accounts and registra-
tions of Japanese citizens and records of communications
sent and

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
- 2 -
2
sent and received by the Japanese Embassy for past
years are perhaps of interest in connection with
further investigations of Japanese activities in
Argentina.
17/
Enclosure no. 17 notes the file of Japanese
registration cards. These cards contain up-to-date
information about Japanese registered with the former
Japanese Consulate, and has been maintained by the
Swiss since they took charge of Japanese interests
in February 1944. The cards are required by the Swiss
Legation in order to continue their routine protection
work for Japanese nationals resident in Argentina.
In preparing the list of Japanese proposed for depor-
tation, in accordance with the Department's confidential
instruction of September 25, 1945, entitled "Submission
of Lists of Obnoxious Japanese for Possible Repatriation",
use was made of the cards in order to obtain up-to-date
information on each person considered. The Offices of
the Legal Attaché and the Military Attaché plan to co-
operate with the..appropriate officers in the British
Embassy in completing the final list to be submitted
within the near future.
The Swiss Legation rented offices at R.S. Peña 636
and moved in on November 19, 1945, in order to continue
its work of protecting Japanese nationals. One room in
these offices is being set aside fer the storage of the
ex-Japanese Embassy and Consular arohives, where examina-
tion may be made by the interested Allied authorities
when they wish. In accordance with instructions from
the Department, the Argentine Foreign Office has been
granted access to the archives for examination.
In addition seven file cabinets were acquired from
the Swiss Legation. Those which have been opened thus
far have contained no records of interest, but mainly
nearly empty file folders and miscellaneous office
equipment.
Respectfully yours,
John M. Cabot
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim
Enclosures:
1-l2, inclusive list contents of 12 wooden boxes
containing mainly books and arohives used by former
Japanese Embassy and Consulate.
13 lists contents of a steel cabinet containing records.
14-16, inclusive list contents of three trunks.
17 concerns Japanese registrations.
Original (for Ozalid machine) to Department.
File no. 820.02.
Gerald Warner: jad

Enclosure no. 14 to Despatoh no:
AIR MAIL
dated December i, 1945, from the
American Embassy at Buenos Aires,
47
Argentina.
Contents of Trunk No. I Entrusted Under Seal by
Ex-Japanese Embassy to Swiss Legation in February
1944. Examined November 30, 1945 by Allied
Authorities.
This trunk contains numerous confidential files
and records of communications sent and received by
the Embassy. A cursory examination of folders nos.
1-28, inclusive (no. 18 being divided into three
parts, a, b, and o) indicates that the folders may
contain information of further interest along the
lines set forth. In addition the trunk contains
thirteen black-bound record books giving lists of
despatches, reports, instructions, and telegrams
sent and received by the Embassy since 1933. These
records give the date, number and subject of each
communioation and a detailed examination might reveal
items of interest.
Folder No. 1
Letter marked secret from the Japanese Legation
in Montevideo, dated September 25, 194l, states that
telegrams in Roman letters for May 1935 had been
destroyed by burning in accordance with instruotions.
Accounts from Montevideo set forth entertainment
expenses for first and second periods for l94l, show-
ing less than l00 pesos per month spent for entertain-
ment.
Secret Embassy report of November 4, 194l, sets
forth Japanese-Uruguayan trade balance as of November
30, 1941.
Buenos Aires Embassy's secret report of Novem-
ber 12, 194l sets forth propaganda expenditures for
September 194l, revealing many payments of interest,
as follows:
a 500-peso subsidy to the newspaper E1
Argentin Djijo, Uspallata 981.
Payment of 70-peso subsidy to E.C.
Gunawardana in connection with his book "Ceylon
y sus Derechos".
$lo0 (pesos) to Transocean for receipt of
Domei news in July.
$50 (pesos) to Japanese lawyer Tsugimaru
Tanoue for services during September.
$l00 (pesos) to Argentine publicist Albino
Pugnalin for servioes in September.
$500 (pesos) to Saporiti News Agency for
September

Folder No. 42 (September-November 1943 and January 1944)
Report dated January 26, 1944 by Ambassador Tomi1
to Shigematsu on "Disposal of the documents and books
of the Japanese Embassy in Argentina and Japanese loss
in Buenos Aires upon breaking of Japanese-Argentine
relations"
I. Embassy
1. Incoming and outgoing copies of
telegrams all destroyed by burning.
2. All copies of incoming and outgoing
secret documents from January 1, 1936 to
present destroyed by burning.
3. All copies of ordinary incoming and
outgoing documents in Japanese and foreign
languages since January 1, 1936 preserved
to be handed over to custody of the pro-
tecting power.
4. Books and magazines connected with
secrecy all burned. Other things of im-
portance to be entrusted to the custody
of the protecting power, and the remainder
discarded.
II. Consulate
1. Copies of incoming and outgoing mes-
sages to and from the Fereign Office since
1935 and copies of incoming and outgoing
messages to and from publio offices abroad
since the opening of the Consulate - all
destroyed by burning.
2. Copies of any documents in clear or
code recognized to be of no future importance
all destroyed by burning.
3. Copies of any documents in clear or
code recognized to be of future importance
(saved) to be entrusted to custody of the
protecting power.
4. Documents and books of importance
for future reference (saved) to be entrusted
to custody of the protecting power and the
balance disoarded.

dated
December 12, 1945, from the American
AIR MAIL
Embassy at Buenos Áires, Argentina.
Contents of Trunk No. III Entrusted Under Seal by
Ex-Japanese Embassy to Swiss Legation in February
1944. Examined November 30, 1945 by Allied
Authorities.
This trunk contains folders nos. C-1 through C-20,
inclusive, containing mainly information of a consular
nature such as registration and passport records; also
six black-bound books consisting of accounts ledgers
and records of communications sent and received by the
Consulate since 1934.
A report of February l944 contained in folder no.
C-l2 indicates that at the time the Japanese handed
over charge of Japanese interests to the Swiss Legation
there were Sooo Japanese residents in Argentina. The
report gives a break-down of the community by employment
as well as by numbers of men, women and children in each
category.
It is believed that a detailed examination of these
records would be of value in obteining information about
members of the Japanese community in Argentina.

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.99)
1557
Enclosure No. 17 to Despatch No.
dated December t, 1945, from the
AIR MAIL
Amerioan Embassy at Buenos Aires,
Argentina.
List of Contents of Inder Card Cabinets Nos. 18-26
Inclusive, entrusted by the Japanese Embassy to the
Swiss Legation, November l944. Examined December 1,
1945 by the Aliied Authorities.
These index cards comprise a ready reference to
the registration and passport status of Japanese nationals
in Argentina. The small cards are filed alphabetically
in chrenological order, the names being written in both
Japanese and Roman letters. The card also sets forth the
date of registration, registration number, passport number,
date of birth, occupation, legal residence in Japan, and
local residence in Argentina together with comments by
the registration offioe. These small cards are kept in
two sections, one of live registrations and the other a
dead registration file. The dead cards are crossed with
a line of red ink.
The live registrations are filed in alphabetical
order (more or less). Cards contain the name of the
applicant in both Japanese and Roman letters and in
addition give the date of birth, passport number, date
of arrival in Argentina and route taken in coming to
this country, permanent residence in Japan, family data,
including names, birth dates, and family relationship,
also local address and dates of change in such address,
and notations of interest by the registering consul.
An examination of a number of names of interest
has revealed information of value in connection with
the investigation of Japanese in Argentina. For
instance, a notation on one registration card states
that the applicant entered Argentina seeretly from
Paraguay, having originally come from Brazil via the
Matto Grosso to Paraguay and crossed the river from
Encarnación to San Ignacio, Province of Misiones,
Argentina.

I believe this suggested draft reply adequately

"The Secretary of State is informed that the
American Legation in Beirut has as a matter of
courtesy informed the French Delegation General

894.414/12-1645
Beirut in connection with the arohives and certain

Gonsulate in that city.

that the archives of the former German Gonsulate
General in Beirut and certain German property in
Lebanon and Syria are held, or sequestered, by
French authorities in Beirut. If this be so,

French Government's views as to the propriety and
desirability of placing such archives and property
under the joint custodianship of the representa-

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
Whatever be the reply which the Department makes
to the French aide memoire, I should appreciate your
sending a copy thereof to the Legation at Beirut,
together with a copy of the aide memoire itself.
I may add, by way of explanation of my two sug-
gested additions to the suggested draft reply, that
my first paragraph is intended to give specifio
answer in the matter of the specifio subjeot (i.e.
Japanese archives in Beirut) which prompted the aide
memoire and that my second paragraph 1s designed to
smoke out an official French statement on the con-
fused question of the continuing activity of the
French "sequestre" in Beirut.
Other papers in the Department's files but not
attached herewith will give you, should you wish to
go further into the matter, the pertinent facts
regarding the relinquishment by the Swiss Consul
in Beirut of the German consular archives formerly
under his oustody.
A further point of considerable interest in this
general connection is that the French "Sequestre"
in Beirut has administered (and presumably still ad-
ministers) considerable official and private Italian
property in Syria and Lebanon. I know, however, of
no instructions in this matter sent by the Department
to the Legation in Beirut. If any further action
by the Legation be desired, I should appreciate
receiving a special instruction on the subject, inolud-
ing citations from any pertinent texts indicative of
joint Allied responsibility in the matter.
George-Wadsworth

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
DRAFT
The Acting Secretary of State presents his compliments to His
Excellency the Ambassador of the French Republic and refers to the
Embassy's Aide Memoire No. 771 of September 24, 1945, in regard to
Japanese diplomatic and consular archives in the Levant, and in regard
to the more general question of Japanese public and private property
situated outside Japan.
With regard to the first question, the action taken by the
American Minister at Beirut was in accordance with instructions issued
him on the basis of he decision of the British, Soviet, Chinese and
United States Governments.
Thesdecision  he beveves was motivated by the
same considerations which prompted the French, British, Soviet and
United States Governments to cause German consular and diplomatic
archives abroad to be placed under their control. In the circumstanaes,
The French Government will doubtless agree that it would be unwise to
alter the decision which has been taken with respect to German consular
and diplomatic archives. If so, it would be difficult to justify
altering the paralel decision with respect to Japan.
With respect to the second question, that of Japanese public
and private property situated outside Japan, no final decision has
yet been reached. Note has been taken, however, of the French desires
in the matter and the Embassy may feel certain that the interests of
all nations which contributed to the defeat of Japan will be given the
most careful consideration.

EMBASSY OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Mexico, D. F., December 18, 1945
CONFIDENTIAL - MAXIMUM PRIORITY
NO. 27,744
SUBJECT:
With Reference to the Disposition of the
Japanese Archives and Property in Mexico.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington, D. C.
Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Embassy's despatch
894.414/12-1845
No. 27,641 of December 10, 1945, and previous correspondence
between the Department and this Embassy with regard to the
disposition of the Japanese archives and property in Mexico.
It will be noted in the despatch above referred to
that a communication was addressed by me to the Swedish
Minister on December 5, 1945, requesting comprehensive in-
formation with regard to Japanese archives, property, assets,
accounts and leases. This information was requested on
behalf of this Embassy and on behalf of the representatives
of Great Britain, China and the Soviet Union.
A reply to my communication has now been received from
the Swedish Minister, in which is contained specific replies
to the questions contained in the letter addressed to the
Swedish Minister on December 5, 1945, which data are self-
explanatory. copies are also enclosed of the protocol dated
July 9, 1945, regarding the taking over by the Swedish
Legation of the Japanese interests in Mexico from the Lega-
tion of Portugal. In addition to the copies of the protocol,
there is also enclosed herewith a copy of a lease executed
by the Chargé d'Affaires of Portugal in Mexico, representing
the interests of Japan in this country, and the leasor, Sr.
CS/VJ
D. José Tazzer, representing himself and brothers, co-
proprietors of the premises which formerly housed the Japa-
nese Legation. Copies of these documents are sent to the
Department for its information.
RespectfullA
yours,
SEP 30 1947
Geor
S. Messersmith
Enclosures (5)
RHGeist:co
In quintuplicate to the Department.

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
(Enclosure to Despatch No. 27,744 of December 18, 1945)
C O P Y
LÉGATION DE SUEDE
México, D.F., December 10th, 1945
3 encl.
Dear Mr. Ambessador:
With reference to your letter of December 5th, 1945,
./. I beg first of all to transmit to you herewith a copy of
the protocol dated July 9th, 1945, with one enclosure, re-
garding the taking over by this Legation of the Japanese
interests in Mexico from the Legation of Portugal. This
protocol will in substance give you information covering
the six points put forward in your letter.
Ad point (1):
The archives of the Japanese Legation in my care thus
only consist of the following items:
2 files conteining demographic documents with regard
to Japanese subjects residing in Mexico.
1 file regarding repatriated Japanese subjects.
1 file regarding interned Japanese subjects.
1 file containing copies of certificates issued by
the Japanese Consulate in Mexico.
2 files regarding passport visas.
3 files of correspondence exchanged between the former
Japanese Legation and the Diplomatic Corps in Mexico,
Mexican authorities, etc.
1 file of correspondence with commercial firms in
Mexico.
1 file of varions letters.
All the above-mentioned files are located in this Lega-
tion's Chancery, Liverpool 57, and are kept in aspecial
plate cabinet, last July handed over to us by the Portu-
guese Legation. In the Chancery of the former Japanese
Legation/
His Excellency

C O PY
ORIGINAL.
CONTRATO DE ARRENDAMIENTO, de la Casa NúmerO 36 (treinta
y seis) de las calles de Orizaba, que celebran, por una parte
como Arrendatario, el Encargado de Negocios de Portugal en
México, Representante de los intereses Japoneses en esta, y
por la otra parte como Arrendador, el Señor D. José Tazzer, en
representación propia y de sus hermanos, Co-propietarios de la
casa a que se ha hecho mención, de acuerdo con las Cláusulas
que a continuación se expresan:
C L A U S U L A S:
I. El Encargado de Negocios de Portugal, así como los
Señores Tazzer, convienen en continuar el Contrato que se había
celebrado con la Legación Japonesa, al mismo precio que por
arrendamiento se pagaba, hasta el mes de febrero de 1943.
II. A partir de marzo 10 de 1943, el precio de arrenda-
miento ascenderá a $975:00, (NOVECIENTOS SETENTA Y CINCO PESOS)
según se conviene en este Contrato.
III. El pago deberá hacerse por mensualidades adelantadas.
IV. El término del arrendamiento, será de dos años forzo-
sos para ambas partes.
V. Cuando cualquiera de las partes, a partir del venci-
miento del Contrato, desee darlo por terminado, tiene la obliga-
ción de notificer este propósito & la otra parte por escrito y
con sesenta dias de anticipación.
VI. Toda mensualidad será pagada íntegra, aún cuando el
Inquilino ocupe la casa parte del mes.
VII. La casa arrendada seré destinada por el Inquilino a
usos de la Misión Diplomática que éste Represente.
VIII. No podrá el Inquilino sin consentimiento escrito
del Arrendador, ceder ó subarrendar la localidad, o parte de
la misma, o destinarla a otros usos.
IX. Tampoco podrá el Inquilino sin consentimiento previo
y por escrito del Arrendador, hacer obras en la localidad, y
las que hiciere contraviniendo lo anterior, quedarán en bene-
ficio de la Finca. Las reparaciones necesarias para la con-
servación del Inmueble, las deberá hacer el Propietario, o se
harán con su consentimiento, las reparaciones de ornato, o
algunas otras que constituyan alguna variación del decorado,
o distribución de la casa, serán por la exclusiva cuenta del
Inquilino. Las reparaciones que deban de hacerse por culpa o
negligencia del Inquilino, deberán ser cubiertas por él
X. El Inquilino recibe la casa aseada y en perfecto
estado, y así mismo deberá devolverla al concluir el arrenda-
miento, obligándose a pagar el Inquilino, cualquier deterioro
que/

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
- 2 -
que haya sido causado por su culpa o negligencia, a excepción
del deterioro natural del uso.
XI. El presente contrato se extiende por duplicado de
acuerdo con lo Dispuesto por el artículo l23, del Reglamento
de la Ley General del Timbre, las matrices de las estampillas
correspondientes, se adhieren al ejemplar principal y los
talones al duplicado.
Las partes Contratantes, perfectamente enteradas del con-
tenido y alcance de todas y cada una de las cláusulas ante-
riores, firman el presente Contrato, y están conformes de que
en su fecha comience a regir.
México, D. F., a 1° de febrero de 1942.
Los Copropietarios:
El Inquilino:
En representación de Attilio
firmado: José Dos Santos
y Antonio Tazzer:
Silva Taveira.
Un sello que dice:
firmado: H. Tazzer.
Legaçao de Portugal, Mexico.

ROYAL EGYPTIAN LEGATION
WASHINGTON, D. C.
The Chargé d'Affaires ad interim of Egypt presents
his compliments to the Honourable the Acting Secretary of
State end has the honour to inform him that Egypt being a
CR
belligerent and an ally of the four Great Powers controlling
Japan is of the opinion that the Egyptian Government, in
701.9483
702.9483
principle, has the right to preserve the archives and properties
of the Japanese Diplomatic and Consular Missions in Egypt,
at the same time permitting access to the Japanese archives
to China, the United St tes of America, Great Britain end
the Union of Soviet Socialist Renublics.
894.414/12-2045
The Egyptien Government, in analogous conditions,
has taken charge of the archives and properties of the German
Diplomatic and Consular Missions in Egypt.
The Swiss Legation in Cairo which protects the
Japanese interests in Egyot has been approached and has in-
formed the Egyotian Government that the Swiss Government has
no objection to the request of the Fgyntien Government
provided the diplomatic renresentatives of the interested
powers agree.
CS/LE
The Chargé d'Affaires would, therefore, appreciate
it if the United States Government would agree and would
instruct the United States Legation in Cairo to bring the
United Stetes Government's acquiescence to the attention of
the Swiss Legation in Cairo.
The Chargé d'Affaires
894.414

=== Page 436 === (confidence 0.98)
CUSTODY OF OFFICIAL JAPANESE PROPERTY
AND RECORDS IN PORTUGAL
I
Establishment of Committee Representing Amerioan,
British and Chinese Missions, and Procedure Adopted.
Plans for taking in custody official Japanese
property in Portugal were made in August 1945 (Seo
Embassy's telegran 1837 August 30, 5 p.m.). Under
that arrangement the Portuguese Government would lend
its good offices to the transfer of Japanese diplomatic
and consular property, provided the Japanese Government
instruct its local Minister to relinquish this property
to the Allied Governments. A committee consisting of
officers of the American, British and Chinese missions
in Lisbon (the Soviet Union has no representation here)
was accordingly constituted.
Only on October 23 (Department's circular telegram
October 23, 5 p.m.) the text of General Mac Arthur's
proposed directive, to be issued October 25, for the
liquidation of Japanese missions abroad, became avail-
able, and the appropriate instructions of the Japanese
Government to its Minister at Lisbon were not issued
until early in November. Final and clear instructions,
according to the Japanese Minister, were received only
on November 12, and on the next day he called at the
Foreign Office and announced the termination of his
mission. On the same day, November 13, the representa-
tives of the three Allied Missions hereafter referred
to as the Committee, accompanied by Mr. Sampaio Garrido
of the Portuguese Foreign Cffice called at the Chancery
of the Japanese Legation, formally taking over from the
Japanese Minister. Guards were posted, the safes and
archives were sealed, and the Committee established its
rules for meetings and consultations for joint action
in the administration of the property. An additional
American member (Mr. Pardillo, a clerk of the American
Embassy) was added to the Committee for the routine
work, and for contact with Mr. Inoue and Mr. Komine,
counselor and the first secretary of the Japanese
Legation, whom the Minister had designated as liaison
in checking the property.
The following sections of this report contain an
account of the activities of the Committee to date.
The Committee's reccinendations or requests for instruc-
tions or authorizations are summarized at the end.
II
Guarding the Premises.
On the first day, November 13, guards, provided
by the European Theater Command, U. S. Army, were posted
in four establishments: the Minister's residence, the
Chancery (two floors in an apartment building), the
Military Attmche's

=== Page 437 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
Military Attache's office, and the Naval Attache's office.
On later inspection it was found that the two latter
establishments contained only office furniture and items
without particular value or confidential character, other
items having been either destroyed or consolidated in
the Legation. Accordingly the guards from these two
premises were withdrawn, and the rooms locked and sealed
pending removal of the property, which has meanwhile been
effected.
It is the Committee's intention, when the residence
and chancery inventories are completed and proper storage
arranged, to communicate with the European Theater Command,
U. S. Army, for the termination of this guard detail,
probably within the next two or three weeks.
III
Archives.
What purported to be the Legation's archives were
found in one steel office cabinet. They contain only
routine correspondence. The Japanese language documents
have been examined by a Jepanese-speaking employee of
the Chinese Legation who reports that he found nothing
of any interest whatever. No cryptographic material of
any character, no accounting books or records, no flags
or insignia, and no political documents, or even registers,
have been found. Questioned on this point, the Japanese
Minlster said that everything of a confidential nature
had been destroyed, by instructions, "immediately after
the Potsdam declaration." It may be said that in the
several weeks at their disposition the Japanese staff
were able to do a good job of cleaning up. The Committee,
assisted by other members of their respective Missions,
have been censtantly on the alert for such documents as
might come to light as the taking of inventory proceeds,
with completely negative results.
In any event, the so-called archives, the seals.
the brass door-plate, and certain card indexes (from
which the more interesting items appear to have been
eliminated) ave been segregated from the other property
and stored in the American Embassy chancery pending final
instructions as to their disposition (see Recommendation
No. 1 at end cf report).
IV
Japanese Representation in Portuguese Colonies.
Sec vo dan
Certain records pertaining to the former Japanese
consulate at Oporto, closed some time ago, were found in
tels
the chancery and consolidated with the Legation archives.
There appears to have bsen no Japanese cfficial establish-
ment elsewhere in Portugal or iortuguese possessions,
except at two points in the Far East, at Timor and Macao.
40
The Japanese Minister said that be had no contact with
MAIOC = 
those offices, but believed that the office at Timor had
already been closed, and that Macao was being liquidated
ah1
under dircct instructions from Tokyo. From the British
Embassy it has been learned that the Britis Consul at
2e
Macao

=== Page 438 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Macao has been authorized to take over official Japanese
property and funds there, in conjunction with a newly-
appointed Chinese official trare. In the lack of direct
American repreaentation at Macao the British Consul rep-
resents American interests there. (See Recommendation No. 2)
American consular offices in Portuguese possessions
have been requssted to report whether there are records
or property in their respective jurisdictions which should
have the Committee's attention.
V
Official Japanese Funds.
An account has bee opened in the Bank of London
and South America, Ltd., in the name of tne three Missions,
to be operated over the joint signature of the members of
the Committee. Into this account has been deposited the
Portuguese currency found in the Japanese Legation safes,
the total deposit being Escudos 26,138,159.40. At the
current rate of exchange (1.00 equal to Escudos 24.68)
this represents a value of"$1,061,108.50
The currency was found in several lots in four
different safes, and according to the Japanese Minister
shoula be segregated for final bookkeeping as follows:
1. Legation cash
Escudos 1,375,080.75
2. Funds of Japanese
Ministry of Educution
22,443.25
3. Funds of the Yokohama
Specle Bank, Ltd.
21,105,374.95
4. Funds of the Tapanese
Militery Attache
1,309,780.45
5. Funds of Lhe Japanese
Naval Attache
2,375.480.00
Total, Escudos
26,188,159.40
The above itemization shows some minor variation
from the figures presented by the Japanese Minister
before the mone was counted. The Naval Attache's cash
Item was found to be 5, 0o0 escuaos less than shown on
the Minister's memorandum, and the Legation's cash item,
claimed to be Escudos 1,377, 021.35, was counted as
Escudos 1,377, 080.75 in the fin5 or preliminary count,
and 1ater found to be Escudos 1,375, 080.75 when the
Bundles of 1,00o escudo notes were broken for counting
the individual notes.
The Japanese Minister requested that particular
noto be made of his reservetion in relinquishing the
funds of the Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd. He saiu that
according to the strict terms of his instructiona these
funds were not "ofricial property" and he requested that
the three Governments be informed that the amount in
question should be credited to the oank in question.
The Committee

=== Page 439 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
The Committee, on the other hand, while insiting
on taking over these funds, has certain questions in
mind, of quite another character; namely, it is trying
to ascertain whether certein remittances of Red Cross
funds may not have been assimilatea to this account.
This will be the subject of a separate report, as the
investigation continues.
In addition to the cash which was physically taken
over, there are three official Japanese bank accounts
in Lisbon, witn the following balances:
Escudos
Legation's account, Banco

Applicetions nave been wade for consolidating these
accounts with the main &ccount in the Bank of London and
South America, but the matter is still pending before
the Portuguese authorities, as the accounts had been
blocked.
VI
Accounting Procedure.
Current minor expenditures for Jepanese account are
being made from the funds as deposited. Vouchers are
obtaIned for each item of expenditure. No salaries or
maintenance costs of Jepanese personnel are involved,
since advances of salary had been made, presumably under
instructions from Tokyo, before the Legetion ceased
functioning. The Committee has no liabillty for expendi-
tures for rent for the quartera of the Military and Naval
Attaches; under the le&se contract for the chancery querters
there is a liability for une nenth's rent only, and since
the quarters are still in use for stcrage and for taking
invontory this is clearly a reasonable Iiability. There
1s, moreover, a prospect for terminating the lease before
its expiration on Jaruary 25, 1e46. No expenditures have
been made under the lease cuntract for the Japanese
Legation residence, which expires only in April 1946.
Details concerning these leases re given under
Section VIlI, Leased Preperty.
The question has arisen in the Committee as to the
attitude of the three Governments in the event of eventual
claims in Portugal, private or official, against the
Japanese Government. The Committee has had no intimation
thus far of any such claims, and would appreciate any
information wnicn the Department might have on this matter,
or any guidance which would be usorul at this stage.
VII
Transfers to Siamese Legation.
The only

=== Page 440 === (confidence 0.98)
~5~
The only accounting item of earlier transactions
made available tc the Committee concerns two auvances
of 200,000 escudos each (tota1 about $16,200) made by
the Japanese Minister, Morito Morishima, to the Legetion
of Thailand (Siam) at Lisbon under date of April 5 and
July 19, 1945. Receipts for these advances, signed by
Colonel Mom Snidvongsseni, the Siamese Minister, were
found in one of the safes and are now in the Committee's
custody. They st&te, in French, that the amounts are
advances "to be reimbursed when the budgot (allotments)
of this Legation shall have been transferred." It is
supposed that the Department will prefer to have this
account aujusted at Tokyo. (See Recommendation No. 3)
VIII
Leased Property.
The Japanese Government owns no real property in
Portugal. The status of the various leases is as follows:
1. Office of Naval Attache: The office quarters
were combined witn oificer's living quarters under a
personal lease contract. All official property has been
inventoried and removed to werelouse; no rent payments
by Committee, a no cutstanding obligations.
2. Office of Military Attache: Rent contract termi-
nated under a clause in the lease, as of November 30, 1945.
Rent for November had been paid in auvance by the Japanese.
All official property inventoried and removed to warehouse;
no outstanding llabilities.
3. Legation Chancery: Two floors in an apartment
building. Termination of leases possible under contract,
as of January 25, 1946. Notice of terminetion given.
Rent for December had been paid in advance by the
Japanese; rent for January paid by Cormittee, under the
month-in-advance clause, the premises being still in use
for storage and for taking inventory. A prospective
tenant, the Canadian Consul General, is available. If
he occupies the quarters before January 25, 1946, in
which case ke would be asked tc make a direct contract
with the owner, a partial refund of rent paid may be
obtained.
4. Legation Residence: This lease can be terminated
as of the end of April 1946; the monthly rent, amounting
to 6, 400 escudos (about #260), has been paid by the
Jepanese up to the end of the current month, December.
The Committee considers it desirable to vacate the premises
at the earliest practicable date, even though some rent
might still have to be paid, in view of the serious
deterioration of parts of the building during the 13 years
of Japanese occupancy. This would fix definitely upon
the Japanese rather than upon the Allied Governments
responsibility for this deterioration, for structural
changes which may have been made, for losses of inventory,
or for damage from the elements, to which such old and
defective buildings are susceptible. There are also
safes and heavy articles of furniture to be removed from
defective parts of the building. It would obviously be
advantageous

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
advantageous to proceed with this promptly. The building
is now in use, for storage and for taking inventory, but
could be vacated during the month of January. This
would leave three months' rent, for February, March and
April, payable under the contract, with little prospect
of a new tenant willing to take over the lease, in view
of the delapidation of the building.
The question has arisen in the Committee whether
the three Governments would consider themselves bound
by a lease contract once the necessity for retaining
the quarters had ended. In the case of German liabilities
in Portugal the principle appears to have been adopted
to repudiate responsibility, beyond the period of use-
fulness to the Allied Governments, for liabilities
incurred before taking over of the premises. In the
opinion of the Committee the two cases are not parallel,
since the Japanese Government is still in being, and
since the Legation was transferred to the three Govern-
ments by the Japanese Minister (who otherwise would have
continued to reside there) on instruction of his Govern-
ment, whereas the German premises had been sealed by the
Portuguese Government. The Committee therefore feels
that it should continue to pay rent under the contract,
while trying to come to terms with the owner for an
earlier termination if possible. The Department's
instructions on this point are requested. (See Recom-
mendation No. 4)
IX
Inventory.
Inventory has been taken of the property in the
offices of the Military and Naval Attaches, and is about
completed in the Legation chancery. At the Minister's
residence much of the furniture and fittings belong to
the owner of the building. These effects have been
segregated and are in process of reconcilement with the
owneris inventory. Glass, china, silver, linen, objets
d'art and part of the books, belonging to the Japanese,
are now being inventoried and packed for storage.
The library, whether at the residence or at the
chancery, is not being inventcried in detail. A careful
examination of these books shows that while some of them,
at the chancery, appear to have been indexed, they are
of no particuler value. The Japanese items appear to
be ordinary reference books; the balance are largely
English or European language publications bought from
local booksellers. Tney are almost entirely wartime
literature of transitory interest, on subjects such as
international finance, economics, wartime biogmaphies
and memoirs, politics, propaganda, etc. Of somewhat
more permanent utility are certain reference works;
these are: Chambers' Encyclopedia, 10 volumes; Hand-
woerterbuch der Staatswissenschaft, 9 volumes; a Portu-
guese Encyclopedia, 1l volumes; and a popular edition
of the Larousse (French) encyclopedia, 6 volumes. No
technical works of special value were found.
Ina

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
In a storeroom of the chancery was found a second-hand
set, judging by an inscription in the first volume, of
the Imperial Gazeteer of India, 26 volumes.
Some 40 cans of films were found. These are being
examined by the service attaches of the three Allied
missions, and will be the subject of a separate report
if they appear to be of particular interest.
Japanese Government owned motor cars (7 passenger
cars, 1 light truck, 1 motorcycle and 2 small trailers)
in varying degrees of mechanical condition, have been
lccated, Inventoried and immobilized, except for two
cars being operated interchangeably, under insurance
which had already been paid by the Japanese, for carrying
food for the guards, changing the guard, and inspections
by tne Committee.
x
Disposition of Property.
As the Japanese leased properties are being vacated
the official property is being stored in a modern,
guarded warehouse (Companhia Geral dos Transportes,
Poço do Bispo 1, Lisboa), at a rental of 42 escudos
(about $1.68) per square meter per month. Space is being
taken only as required. Thus far only the effects from
the offices of the Military and Naval Attaches have been
so stored. Packing charges are being kept at a minimum,
in view of the assurance of the Japanese Minister himself
that the official property in Lisbon is quite without
historical or artistic value. Only the glass, china
and silverware and some miscellaneous small items have
been packed with particular care, the silverware in
sealed cases. It is the Committee's recommendation that
all of the Japanese property, with the possible excep-
tion of the so-called archives (see Section lII) be
disposed of by sale. The factors taken into consideration
in formulating this reccmmendation are (1) the lack of
any Japanese-owned building for storage, and the very
considerable expense of storage in a commercial warehouse;
(2) uncertainty as to the time when the Japanese Govern-
ment may again be in a position to re-establish a mission
in Portugal, and the probability that such a mission
would be on a much smaller scale; (3) the fact that
everything appears to have been bought locally, for
purely utilitarian purposes; and (4) the complete lack
of anything of historical or artistic value, in the
opinion of the Committee and as specifically confirmed
by tne Japanese Minister himself. The office equipment,
for example, is all of inferior quality; at the residence
there is practically no linen; the glassware is of quite
ordinary quality, the china service (plates of various
sizes) a standard good quality white china with gold
band, without monogram or insignia; the silver flat ware
real silver, local manufacture, with plain monogram "L J";
the serving dishes, part plated and part real silver,
local manufacture. The cerpets, mostly of local manu-
facture, of ordinary quality and somewhat worn. As
already

=== Page 443 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
already mentioned, most of the furnishings of the Logation
residence belong to the owner of the building. Consequently
& complete liquidation of the Japanese property here would
appear to be the most economical and practical procedure.
Certain small odd supplies of perishable foodstuffs, un-
suitable for storage and already deteriorating, have
been given to the British hospital; a small lot of local
wine and imported whisky, the value written off, and
uninventoried by tho Japanese, has been inventoried by
the Committee which proposes to dispose of it by sale.
The contents of an unaerground gasoline tank, estimated
at some 5-6,O0c liters, synthetic product, will be turned
back to the dealer, and the proceeds credited to the
Conmittee's account. A considerable volume of old news-
papers, magazines, propaganda material, etc. will be
disposed of by sale as wastepaper. (For ail items men-
tioned above see Reconmenuaticn No. 5)
XI
Repatriation.
Due note has been taken of tne Department's views
1:
(telegram no. 1773 cf November 9) concerning the eventual
repatriation of Japanese personmnel from the several
(European posts where such personmel remain. As of this
date, the situation is that the British Foreign Office
has asked for information concerning the personnel in
Lisbon, noting, however, that this is not to be inter-
preted as an indication that any actual plans were being
formulated, or that there is early prospect of repatriation
for this group.
According to the Japanese Minister, the personnel
in question number 47, including 6 womsn nd 8 children
of ages between 1 yoar and 15 years. The Minister hes
stated that befere closing his misslon he paid advances
of sslary for one yeer, presumably under instructions
from Tokyo  d presumably up to November 1946. It is
therefore unlikely that the Committee wi ll be called
upon for any oxpenuitures for subsistence if the re-
patriation can be arranged within the next few months.
While there is ne record that any of these individuals
come under the "obnoxious" category, their early repat-
riation from Portugal would clearly be desirable, par-
ticulerly since, as a resul (f the circumstances in
which the mission was closed while Portuguese represen-
tarion at Tokyo continues, the Portuguese are inclined
to accord tc the Japanese Minlster and his staff, in
the interval perding their departure, a certain informal
courtesy status.
KII
Personal Effects of Personnel Already Departed.
The Japanese Minister has handed to the Committee
a list of 17 Japanese personnel, formerly serving in
Portugel but transferred to other posts or returned to
Japan, during the last few years, whose personal luggege
had been lert in his custody. This is not strictly
spetking "official property" but the Committee felt
nevertheless

=== Page 444 === (confidence 0.98)
-9-
nevertheless that it should take those effects into
custody. The effects, chiefly traveling bags said to
represent tlhe excess luggage of persons obliged to
travel light by airplane, comprise also a few cases
of the personal property of Mr. Saburo Kurusu, formerly
Minister here, later Anoassador at Berlin and understood
now to be in Tokyo. All these effects are being tagged
for identification, and will be placed in storage with
the official property, but it is proposed to release
them to the Japanese Minister, at the time of his
repatriation, for return to Jepan. Nearly all the
containers are locked, the keys being held presumably
by their respective owners. (See Recommendation No. 6)
XI1I
Japanese Consul at Perpignan.
It has come tc the Conmittee'a knowledge that
Mr. H. Takawa, formerly Japanese Consul at Marseilles,
is now at Perpignan, France, close to the Spanish frontier.
The Committee proposes thet inquiry be made at Paris
as to the circumstances of his continued residence in
France, and tiat errangements oe made for nis assimi-
letion to some other Japtnese group, at Madrid for
example, pending repatriation. (See Recommendation No. 7)
XIV
Recommendations.
In the light of the preceding discussion the Committee
makes the following reconinendations:
1. Archives. (See page 2) Po be held in custody at
the chancery of the Anerican Embassy pending authorization
from Tokyo either for their destruction (preferred since
all inportant documents appear to have been already elimi-
nated), or for their shipment to Japan. Volune, about
two ordinary shipping ceses.
2. Status of Japanese Consulates at Timor and Maceo.
(See page 2) It is suggested that the vepartment confirm
threugh Scai whether these two officos have been, or are
in process of being liquidatod, es reported, 'and provide
further instructions if the Committee at Lisbon can be
helpful in this connection.
3. Advances to Siamese Legation. (See page 5) 1t
would appear advisable to adjust this account between
the Governments concerned, as an iten in the settlement
between Japan and Siam. Does the Department wish to
have the Siamese Minister's receipts forwarded to Washington?
4. Continued Rent Payment for Japanese Legation
Residence. (See pe ge 6) That the Committee be authorized,
if unable to come to terms with the owner of the building
for terminating the lease before its normal date of
expiration, hpril 30, to pay the rent for February,
March and April (the building will still be in use in
January) as stipulateu in the contract.
5. Disposition

=== Page 445 === (confidence 0.98)
-10-
5. Disposition of Japanese Property by Public Sale.
(See page 7) That the Committee be authorized to arrange
for public ssle, along the general lines adopted for the
disposition of official German property in Lisbon, of
all the moveable property taken into custody by the
Committee, specifically, the motor cars, office equipment
and furniture, radios, electrical applicances, living
quarters furnishings, liquors (imported items to be sold
to persons having free entry privileges), silver, glass
and china. The books likewise to be sold, unless it is
desired to keep the reference books listed on page 6,
and unless there would be objection to disposing of books
containing the rubber stamp of the Japanese Legation.
6. Disposition of Effects of Personnel Already
Departed. (See page 9) That the Committee be authorized
to release to the custody of the former Japanese Minister
at the time of his repatriation to Japan, the luggage
said to be the property of personnel formerly stationed
in Portugal, out transferred to other posts or already
01
returned to Japan; and that the inspection of such
property be made upon arrival in Japan.
7. Disposition of Japanese Consul at Perpignan,
France.(See page 9) That the Department inform the
Embassy at Paris of the presence of this Japanese official
in France, with whatever instructions may be ap propriate
with & view to essimilating him to some other Japanese
14
group for eventual repatriation.
8. Consideration of Claims. (See page 4) That the
Committee be informed of any policy which may have been
adopted for the consideration of any claims against the
Japanese Government, private or official, which may
arise in Portugal, in the process of liquidating Japanese
property located here.
In working out the Committee's accounting procedure
it has been observed that in the final disposition of
Japanese property and personnel considerable expense
may be incurred for official telegrams to the respective
Governments. The Committee would like to be informed
whether such telegrams would be considered a proper
charge against the Japarese account, to be paid from
Japanese funds at the Committee's disposition, rather
than be paid, as hitherto, from the funds of the respective
Missions.
9. Funds of the Yokohama Speeso Bank. (See page 3)
That the Committee be informed whether the Department
desires to accord any separate and particular treatment
to the funas allegedly belonging to tho Yokohama Specie
Bank, Ltd. and presently held on deposit together with
other Japanese funds.

CONFIDENTIAL
To the

Lisbon.
The Seoretary of State refers to the Embassy's
despatoh no. 615 of December 21, 1945 concerning
Allied custody of offioial Japanese arohives and
property in Portugal.
The Department approves the procedure followed
by the Embassy, together with the other Allied Missions
at Lisbon, in taking over Japanese arohives and property
there. For the Embassy's guidance in connection with the
894.414/12-21
nimespecific points raised at the conclusion of its
despatch under reference, the Department has the follow-
XR
ing comments to make:
701.9453
1. Arohives. It is believed that the instruotions con-
tained in Department's ciroular telegram of January 31,
1946 will already have answered the Embassy's questions
in this regard. It should be noted that sanotion eannot
be given to the destruction of any arohives.
2. Status of Japanese Consulates at Macao ahd Timor.
Since the Department is replying by telegraph to the
Embassy's telegram no. 235 of Mareh 4, 1946 on this question,
separate instruotions are not inoluded here.
3. Advances to Siamese Legation. The advances to the Siamese
Legation eannot be settled in Lisbon, and consequently the
receipts should be forwarded here.
Continued Rent Payment for Japanese Legation Premises.
S
The Department approves the Embassy's recommendation
that the premises in question be vacated at the earliest
possible moment, although it may be necessary to continue
rent payments until the expiration of the present lease.
Funds for this purpose are in the Department's opinion
properly chargeable to the joint trust account whioh the
Embassy states was opened with the Bank of London and South
America, Ltd., in the name of the three Allied Missions.
5. Disposition of Japanese Property by Publie Sale. Japanese
official property should if possible be stored. However,
in cases where storage of such property is impractioable due
to excessive quantities, disproportionute expense, er the
danger of depreciation or deterioration, sale may be made
and the proceeds thereof deposited in the joint trust ascount
It must be emphasizsd that this provision does not apply
either to official archives or to the prdvate property of
DCR NE Unit
na
former
Uat.
Dist

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
fermer Japanese personnel.
6. Disposition of Effects of Personnel already De
The Department assumes that at the time the fe
Japanese Minister left Lisbon the Committee followed it
own suggestion of raleasing to his eustody baggage be
If such baggage still remains in Portugal it may be store
with the official Japanese property mentioned above in
numbered paragraph five.
7. Disposition of the Former Japanese Consul at Perpignan.
This would no longer appear to be a problem since the
gentleman in question is said to have departed with other
Japanese officials on the Plus Ultra.
8. Consideration of Claims. The Department is not yet
in a position to advise regarding claims against the
Japanese Government. It is expected that the proposed
Reparations Commission to be established under the Far
Eastern Commission, will pass upon the poliey governing
such claims.
With regard to the Committee's inquiry coneerning
telegrapht charges incurred in connection with the sustody
of Japanese property and archives, the Embassy is informed
that in the opinion of the Department sueh eharges are
properly payable with funds drawn from the joint trust
account already established with the Bank of London and
South America. Generally speaking, withdrawals may be
made from this account to meet custodial expenses incurred
in connection with Japanese property, including charges for
rent, moving, storage, communications, eto., and, if oceasion
develops, for the relief of indigent Japanese nationals
through a proteeting Power. It may bc added that in
questions of this sort the Department is content to rely
fully on the Committee's discretion and good judgment.
9. Investigation in Japan of the operation of the
Yokohama Specie Bank funds reveals that there is no
reason to accord them any special treatment on taking
them over, and they should be handled in the same way
as Government funds.

=== Page 451 === (confidence 0.99)
AIR MAIL
COPY
My dear Colleague,
With reference to the question of the Japanese
archives, I have now received a telegram from the
Foreign Office stating that unless local conditions
make it impracticable, my Government agree to these
archives being transferred to Washington for examina-
tion. At the same time they must reserve the right
both of any Foreign Office representative whom they
may appoint or of tne British Security Coordination,
to have free access to the documents in Washington at
all times.
2. As rerards inmovable controlled property, they
are of the opinion that where Japanese diplomatic or
consular buildings or offices are rented, the lease
should be terminated as soon as practicable. I under-
stand that there are no buildings here which are the
property of the Japanese Government, but if there are
my Government consider that they should be sold or put
up for lease, depending on local conditions, and that
the proceeds should be put into an account to be admin-
istered, pending its disposal, by representatives of
Great Britain, the United States, the USSR and China.
In this connection it appears to me that the lst of
July, the date already agreed u on, is most practicable.
3. My Government further consider that official
furniture and movable property other than archives,
stamps and seals, should be sold, the proceeds being
paid into the account referred to in paragraph 2 above.
Stamps and seals, if any, should be treated as archives.
4. Furthermore is Majosty's Government consents
that by agreement between yourself, the Chinese and
JSSR representatives and myself, and the Swedish
Linister, the latter may retain from existing Japanese
funds or from those realized by the sale or lease of
property such amounts as may be necessary for the
continued protection of Japanese nationals.
With kindest regards,
Yours very sincerely,
J. H. Leche
His Excellency
Mr. Claude G. Bowers,
Ambassador to the United
States of America,
Santiago.

SUBJECT:
Transmitting the first installment of a trans-
lation intc English of the Japanese Minister's

TIE HONORABLE
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,

WASHINGTON.
DCL
SIR:
With reference to my secret despatch No. 13,047 lof
November 16, 1945, regarding the Japanese diplomatfc
property and archives in Santiago, I have the honor to
enclose a translation of the first part of a diary written
by Yamagata, former Japanese Minister to Chile, which
894.414/12-2845
was found in a chest in the Swedish Legation upon the
examination of the Jepanese archives stored there.
Reference is made to the notation in the diary under
the date of September 15, 194l: "Reed in German Embassy
a copy of American note to Chilean Government". Attention
is also invited to the remark said to have been made on
701.
February 9, 1942, by the German Ambassador to the Japanese
Minister to the offect that on the previous day the inter-
view between the American Ambassador and Rossetti had
been principally about the matter of copper prices.
Apropos of these two entries in the diary, I consider
that the following observations are necessary. At that
time Juan Sautista Rossetti was the Minister for Foreign
Affairs. I have been informed by Señor Fernández, the
present Minister for Fereign Affeirs, that on the dates
above referred to the Foreign Office was honeycombed with
German partisans and that Fernando ORREGo Vicuña, who was
Chief of Protocol of the Foreign Office, and his assistant,
Regulo VALENZUELA Vera, were notoriously pro-Nazi and in
intimate contact with the German Ambassador. It is quite
possible that the American note was passed cn to the
German Embassy by either Orrego or Valenzuela. It is
likewise possible that Orrego or Valenzuela, or Rossetti
C
himself, conveyed to the German Ambassador the information
S
concerningmy conversation with Rossetti on February 8, 1942
Orrego is now Counselor of the Chilean Embassy in
uenos Aires and Valenzuela was First Secretary in that
1946
Bmbassy until about six months aso, when he was returmed
to Chile because of illness. According to my infoimatlon,
the
MAR 1 2 1946
风

=== Page 485 === (confidence 0.98)
AIR MAIL
-2-
upon the termination of his duties as Assistant Chief
of Protocol Valenzuela was assigned as First Secretary
of the Chilean Embassy at Rio de Jeneiro but the Chilean
Ambassador there, Selñor Gabriel GONZALEZ Videla, re-
fused to have him on his staff becaase he was known to
be pro-Hazi. I consider the assignment of Orrego and
Valenzuela to Buenos Aires significant.
This  art of the former Japanese Minister's diary
is dated from August 28, 1941, through March 31, 1942.
The translation was dene by Mr. Su Shih Chen, Second
Seeretary of the Chinese Legation, and further trans-
lations will be submitted to the Department as they are
finished oy Mr. Chen. A copy of the enclosed translation
has been given the ritish Embassy.
Meanwhile, it is again recommended that the small
amount of Jepanese archives liscovered in the Swedish
Lagation be taken by United States military airplane
to Hashington for examination.
Respectfully yours,
Clann ursowrs

=== Page 496 === (confidence 0.99)
RESTRICTED
Enclosure no. 2 to despatch no. 3879
from American Embassy, Rio de Janeiro,
December 29, 1945
December 27, 1945
Re:
ARCHIVES JAPANESE EMBASSY
Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
On November 16, 1945, the following representatives of
the American, Chinese, and British Embassies assumed control
of the archives of the Japanese Embassy at Rio de Janeiro,
Brazil, from the wedish Legation:
Mr. PAUL C. DANIELS,
Counselor of the American Embassy
Mr. TAN PAU TUAN,
Counselor of the Chinese Embassy
Mr. JOHN DEE GREENVAY,
Counselor of the Pritish mbassy
Present also were Mr. HEIToR TIGRE representing the Brazilian
Foreign Office, Mr. JAN STENSTROM, First Secretary of the
Swedish Legation, together with his Aide, Mr. THoRD A. J.
BENGSTON, and Mr. KENNETH LE SUEUR of the British Embassy.
The archives of the Japanese Embassy were formally turned over
by Mr. RAGNAR KUMLIN, Minister of the Swedish Legation, to the
representatives of the above mentioned Embassies.
It is to be noted that the Japanese Embassy was closed
early in February, l942, and was taken into protective custody
by the Spanish Embassy on June 29, 1942. The Swedish Legation
received responsibility from the Spanish Embassy on May 24, 1945.
The Japanese Embassy is located at Rua Laranjeiras 192 and
consists of a large main building with grounds and a smaller
office building in the rear which was used as the office of the
Naval Attaché. All other functions of the Embassy were carried
on in the main building.
All keys and combinations to the cabinets in the posses-
sion of the Swedish Legation concerning the archives of the
Embassy were turned over to representatives of the American
Embassy, acting jointly on behalf of the British and Chinese
Embassies as well, at which time the various cabinets were
opened after the seals placed thereon by the Swedish Legation
had been broken.
Subsequently, all items located in the Embassy having to
do with the archives were reviewed and microfilm photographs
were taken of index cards representing a record of the Japanese
registered with the Embassy. A relatively extensive library
in Japanese, Portuguese, French, and English was located, and
photographs were taken of the title pages of the books. The
services of a representative of the Chinese Embassy who under-
stood Japanese were available as an aid to ascertaining the
nature of the various records located.
As
RESTRICTED

=== Page 497 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
As a result of this review no items of apparent interest
with respect to intelligence matters were located. Repre-
sentatives of the Offices of the Military Attaché and Naval
Attaché assisted in the review of these archives. Inasmuch
as a possibility existed that records had been crated, all of
the crates located in the Embassy were opened and examined:
No records were located, the contents consisting of dishes,
silver, and other household effects.
On November 28, 1945, Mr. SUETAKA HAYAO, Japanese Consul,
with representatives of the British and Chinese Embassies and
Swedish Legation, visited the Japanese Embassy and succeeded in
opening two of the safes which up to that time had not been
opened. It is noted that a large majority of the filing cabi-
nets and safes were completely empty, and it was apparent that
ample time had been available to destroy any important records.
Mr. HAYAo upon being questioned stated that there definitely
had never been a radio transmitting station or photographic
dark room located in the Japanese Embassy and that the services
of a commercial photographer had been utilized for any photo-
graphic work required. At that time he offered his services to
aid in any manner the examination of records located at the
Embassy. The Consul is married to a Brazilian and has been
stationed in Brazil a number of years.
Through the cooperation of the offices of the United
Artists Studios, Rua Alvaro Alvim 52, Rio de Janeiro, the quan-
tity of 35 millimeter motion picture film was reviewed. All
of this film consists of Japanese propaganda film, mostly with
sound tracks in French, Portuguese, English, and Japanese. The
subject matter of the film covers merchandise of Japan, travelogs
on Japan, discussion of the "China incident", and other such
related matters.
At the present time all of the cabinets in the Embassy
have not been opened since all of the keys could not be located
by the Swedish Legation or the combinations to the safes were
not known. There still remain to be opened two steel safes,
four steel filing cabinets, two small upright wooden cabinets,
and two large upright steel cabinets with combination locks.
RESTRICTED

Enclosure to Despatch No. 3904 of
January 4, 1946 from the Embassy
at Rio de Janeiro, Brazil.
Copy
Rio de Janeiro, Brazil
January 2, 1946
Memorandum for the Chargé d'Affaires
Re:
Archives - Japanese Embassy
For the completion of your files the following
information is furnished:
On December 21, 1945, after telephonic communi-
cation with Mr. John Dee Greenway, Counselor of the
British Embassy, it was decided that there was no
objection to the shipping to Washington, D.C., of a
quantity of motion picture film and other items ob-
tained at the Japanese Embassy in Rio de Janeiro at
the time the archives were taken over on November
16, 1945.
Accordingly, approximately 51 cans of 35 milli-
meter motion picture film, mostly with sound track,
consisting of propaganda on Japanese industry and
related subjects together with about 50 unused
Japanese passports and a quantity of books and pam-
phlets describing Japanese culture, et cetera, will
be forwarded to Washington in the near future by
this office.
It is understood that these items will be avail-
able in Washington to representatives of the Chinese
and British Embassy should they so desire. Mr. Tan
Pau Tuan, Counselor of the Chinese Embassy, has pre-
viously given his approval of the shipment of these
items to Washington to representatives of this office.
Respectfully,
Heber M. Clegg,
Legal Attache

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.99)
AIRGRAM SENT
Department of State
NO.
Washington, SECRET
TIME
Jan 8, 1946
781
9:05 A.M.
CIRCULAR AIRGRAM
TO ALL AMERICAN DIPLOMATIC MISSIONS IN THE
OTHER AMERICANREPUBLICS.
Referring to Department's circular telegram of
October/23, 5 p.m., the Department understands that
"property" of Japanese diplomatic missions now in the
custody of protecting powers includes cash and liquid
894.414/1-846
resources of all kinds whether local currency or others
formerly in the possession of Japenese missions. All
such funds, whether public funds of the mission or
private funds belonging to the individual members of
402
the mission, are to be handed over by the protecting
powers to the Allied diplomatic representatives on
behalf of the four Allied powers. These representa-
tives are responsible for the control of such funds
and are accountable for them; consequently it is neces-
sary that these funds be handed over in their entirety.
However, recognizing the need for certain expenditures
by the protecting powers in continuing routine functions
the Department recommends that such funds be kept in a
trustee account with arrangements for access and accounta-
bility by the protecting powers. Since circumstances
will differ in varying localities it is not the Depart-
ment's intention to describe the procedure in minute
detail but will leave the method to be determined by
the individual missions. It will be recognized that
the objectives to be gained are (l) effective control
of all Japanese public assets; and (2) practicable
arrangement for authorization for necessary expenditures
by the protecting power.
For your general information the Department does
not now anticipate that any transfer of funds will be
made from one country to another in the event that funds

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.97)
AIRGRAM SENT
Department of State
NO.
Washington,SECRET
TIME
-2-
are exhausted locally. If this should happen the pro-
tecting power should apply to the Japanese Government
for replenishment. Department's present instructions
do not authorize extraordinary expenditures such as
repatriation, which will be subject to further study
and advice. Funds which are clearly identified as be-
longing to individuals should be used in preference to
public funds in allocations for purely personal expenses
of the owner.
The trust account may also include the proceeds
of sale of any items of Japanese Government property
which are impracticable to store, perishable or present
in excessive quantities.
It is requested that the Department be informed of
the procedure adopted and the amounts involved in each
case, together with an estimate of future requirements.
It will be necessary that a quarterly report be made
beginning with the situation as of December 31, 1945.
In future reports each mission should indicate the pur-
pose for which withdrawals were made. The Department
considers, for your guidance, that the following types
of expenses are properly allowable: (1) relief to
indigent nationals; (2) fixed expenses of protecting
powers incidental to routine operations; and (3) charges
for moving and storage.
Bynes
